Thermal Conductivity and Theory of Inelastic Scattering of Phonons by Collective Fluctuations
Léo Mangeolle, Leon Balents, Lucile Savary

Abstract
We study the intrinsic scattering of phonons by a general quantum degree of freedom, i.e. a fluctuating “field” Q Q , which may have completely general correlations, restricted only by unitarity and translational invariance. From the induced scattering rates, generalizing the model studied in a companion paper, Ref. Mangeolle et al. 2022 , we obtain the consequences on the thermal conductivity tensor of the phonons. We confirm that, even within our generalized model, the off-diagonal scattering rates involve a minimum of three- to four-point correlation functions of the Q Q fields, and discuss the “semiclassical” vs “quantum” nature of all contributions. We obtain general and explicit forms for these correlations which isolate the contributions to the Hall conductivity, and provide a general discussion of the implications of symmetry and equilibrium; this elaborates on, and extends, the results of Ref. Mangeolle et al. 2022 . We also extend the discussion and evaluation of these two- (diagonal scattering) and four-point correlation functions, and hence the thermal transport, for the illustrative example of an ordered two dimensional antiferromagnet, where the Q Q field is a composite of magnon operators arising from spin-lattice coupling, and confirm numerically that the results, while satisfying all the necessary symmetry restrictions, lead to non-vanishing scattering and Hall effects. In particular, we investigate, both analytically and numerically, the dependence of such intrinsic scattering on a crucial parameter – the magnon to phonon velocity ratio υ \upsilon . We in particular confirm that within some range of υ \upsilon of order 1 the skew-scattering mechanism leads to comparable thermal Hall conductivity for thermal currents within and normal to the plane of the antiferromagnetism, and discover that the temperature scaling of the longitudinal conductivity displays a threshold effect and a non-universal, continuous variation of the scaling exponent with υ \upsilon .



I
Introduction

Two-point correlation functions are ubiquitous
in the study of condensed matter systems. They are often the building blocks of
response functions in scattering and other experiments and appear in
Feynman diagrams, as well as Monte Carlo simulations. They are the central elements of linear response
theory, as is evident from Kubo’s formula
Luttinger 1964
;
Chen et al.
. They are often independent
of the arbitrary phase choice of the wave function.

Higher-order correlation functions have witnessed renewed interest
recently. They arise theoretically in the measurement of
chaos. A particular type of four-point correlation function, the
“out-of-time-ordered” correlator, has been
shown to be related to the Lyapunov exponent, which measures the
rate at which the result of a measurement diverges after a weak initial
perturbation
Swingle 2018
. Multi-point correlations also naturally describe non-linear response, e.g. in non-linear optics such as
second harmonic generation, and in “multi-dimensional
spectroscopy”
Wan and Armitage 2019
. They may
also arise in scattering
measurements at resonance, such as RIXS

Ament et al. 2011
;
Savary and Senthil 2015
. From a statistical point of view, higher
order correlation functions measure the non-Gaussianity of the
distribution of an observable. The more strongly correlated a state
is, i.e. the more it deviates from a free-particle description, the
more significant the non-Gaussianity. Hence multi-point functions are
essential harbingers of strong correlations.

In a companion paper
Mangeolle et al. 2022
, we present the study of
the thermal conductivity due to phonons
linearly
coupled to
another degree of freedom, for example an electronic or a magnetic
one. We summarize the results of that
paper in this paragraph. First, it is demonstrated that this coupling
induces two types of scattering of phonons: those which are symmetric
in the sense of respecting detailed balance, and those which are
antisymmetric and obey an “anti-detailed balance” relation. Only
the latter “skew scattering” events contribute to a thermal Hall
effect of phonons, as proven by formulating and solving the associated
Boltzmann transport equations. Finally, the results are applied to an
example calculation of the diagonal and Hall components of the thermal
conductivity for the case of a two-dimensional antiferromagnet.

The purpose of the present paper is to extend the problem of
Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
to the most general case, and to give full
detail of the corresponding scattering contributions and their
derivation. We allow the phonons to be both linearly and quadratically
coupled to the fluctuating degree of freedom,
i.e. with an interaction Hamiltonian density (c.f. Eq. (
16
))

\displaystyle H^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\mathbf{k}}\sum_{q=\pm}a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}},

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{n\mathbf{k}\neq n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}=\pm}a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}a_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

(1)

where
a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{-}\equiv a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\vphantom{\dagger}},a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{+}\equiv a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}
are the phonon
annihilation and creation operators for the
n^{\rm th}
phonon mode
with momentum
\mathbf{k}
, and
Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}
,

Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
are the collective fluctuating
“fields” coupled to the phonons (we discuss even more
general forms in the Appendices). The quantity
N_{\rm uc}
is the
number of unit cells in the sample. We provide a full discussion of
all the different scattering contributions generated by these terms
(up to quartic order in the phonon coupling, see Sec.
III.4
), and give a
full exposition of the expressions of the corresponding rates in terms
of correlation functions. We also give a thorough discussion of the
consequences of symmetries and detailed-balance relations on the Hall
conductivity, and show in particular that the conclusion that two-point
correlations functions do not contribute to a Hall effect, arrived at in
Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
for the linear coupling model, continues to
hold in full generality.

Up to fourth order in
\lambda
, where
\lambda
captures the size of
the coupling between one phonon and one
Q
operator, and terms
involving
p
phonon operators are assumed to be of order
\lambda^{p}
,
the longitudinal scattering rate is

D_{n\mathbf{k}}=D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(1)}+D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(2)}+\breve{D}_{n\mathbf{k}}

(2)

where
D^{(1)}
and
D^{(2)}
are obtained in our perturbative
expansion at orders
\lambda^{2}
and
\lambda^{4}
, respectively, and

\breve{D}_{n\mathbf{k}}
encompasses contributions due to other
scattering processes as well as higher-order terms of the expansion.

The skew scattering rate can be similarly expanded in terms at
different orders in
\lambda
. At fourth order, we find the full set
of scattering rates generalizing the results of
Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
to include the two-phonon couplings and
quantum interference terms:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle[Q^{-q}_{n\mathbf{k}},Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}]\{Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}\}\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle\{Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{-q},Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\}\{Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}\}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\left\langle[Q^{-q}_{n\mathbf{k}},Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}][Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}]\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[2];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{-q-q^{\prime}}Q_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}q}\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{-q,-q^{\prime}}[Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}]\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{-q,-q^{\prime}}\{Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}\}\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[2,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{-q}\{Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{-q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{qq^{\prime}}\}\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\left\langle Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{-q}\left\lgroup Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{-q^{\prime}},Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q},Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}\right\rgroup\right\rangle.

(3)

The full scattering rate is the total of all the contributions, summed
over
\sigma=\pm 1=\oplus,\ominus
,
q,q^{\prime}=\pm 1
, and the

[\cdot],[\cdot^{\prime}]
indices which denote the “internal” states of the
scattering process and will be explained in
Sec.
III.3.1
. The
\left\lgroup\cdot,\cdot^{\prime},\cdot^{\prime\prime}\right\rgroup

notation in the last term is explained in
Appendix
C.5
. The first term,
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is that discussed in Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
.

Most importantly, we have separated the processes into those which
satisfy detailed (
\sigma=1
) and “anti-detailed” (
\sigma=-1
)
balance relations,

\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}=\sigma~e^{-\beta(q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}~\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\sigma,-q-q^{\prime}},\quad\sigma=\pm\,{\rm or}\,\oplus,\ominus.

(4)

When these rates are used as input to the Boltzmann equation, we
observe that
only the anti-detailed balance terms can generate a
thermal Hall effect.
A discussion of these (anti-)detailed balance
relations can be found in Secs.
III.3.2
,
IV.1.2
.

After the derivation of these relations and their relation to the
thermal conductivity tensor, and a discussion of the consequences of
symmetry for the latter, we turn to the specific problem of the
antiferromagnet introduced in Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
. Notably, we
significantly extend the treatment there to unveil the dependence of
the thermal conductivity upon the ratio

\upsilon=v_{\rm m}/v_{\rm ph}
of the magnon and phonon velocities,
which is striking and non-trivial. In particular
\kappa_{\rm L}

has the scaling behavior

\kappa_{\rm L}\sim\begin{cases}T^{-1}&\mbox{for }\upsilon<3\\
T^{3-8(\upsilon-1)^{-1}}&\mbox{for }\upsilon>3\end{cases},

(5)

exhibiting a threshold effect and a
non-universal,
continuous
variation of the scaling exponent
with
\upsilon
, as shown in Fig.
1
.
Furthermore, we find that the Hall resistivity dramatically decreases
as
\upsilon
increases, due to the
reduction of the allowed phase space for scattering.

Figure 1
:
Dependence upon
\upsilon=v_{\rm m}/v_{\rm ph}
of the longitudinal
conductivity as a function of
temperature, in log-log scale, for
\breve{D}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\gamma_{\rm ext}=10^{-6}(v_{\rm ph}/\mathfrak{a})
. We highlight the change of scaling behaviors for

T>T_{\lambda}^{\star}
(defined in Eq. (
100
)) at
v_{\rm m}/v_{\rm ph}=3
, above which the
temperature scaling exponent of
\kappa_{\rm L}
is a continuous
function of
v_{\rm m}/v_{\rm ph}
, see Eq. (
5
) or Eq. (
117
).

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
We first (Secs.
II
,
III
)
provide an expanded derivation of the skew and longitudinal
scattering rates,
including those terms which result from
“higher-order”
Q
-phonon interactions and are not present in
Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
. We then (Sec.
IV
) provide a detailed
discussion of the consequences of symmetries and “detailed
balance”-like relations on the Hall conductivity. The final section (Sec.
V
) is an
application of the results to an ordered antiferromagnet, as in
Ref.
Mangeolle et al. 2022
, which we expand on in considerably more
detail, both regarding the longitudinal and skew scattering rates. We obtain
analytical results for the longitudinal conductivity
\kappa_{L}
and
Hall resistivity
\varrho_{H}
in terms of multidimensional integrals,
whose scaling we analyze and verify through numerical
evaluation. Seven appendices provide all details and further
generalizations not given in the main text.

II
Setup

II.1
Derivation

The quasiparticle nature of phonons justifies
treating their dynamics within the Boltzmann equation,

\partial_{t}\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\boldsymbol{v}_{n\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}_{\mathbf{r}}\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}[\{\overline{N}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\}],

(6)

where
N_{n\mathbf{k}}({i_{p}})=\langle i_{p}|a^{\dagger}_{n\mathbf{k}}a^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{n\mathbf{k}}|i_{p}\rangle
is the number of
(n,\mathbf{k})

phonons (
\mathbf{k}
is the phonon momentum and
n
an extra phonon
label, containing the band index and polarization) in the
|i_{p}\rangle
state,
\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{i_{p}}N_{n\mathbf{k}}(i_{p})p_{i_{p}}
is the average population, and

\boldsymbol{v}_{n\mathbf{k}}={\boldsymbol{\nabla}}_{\mathbf{k}}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
,
with
\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
the dispersion of phonons,
is the group velocity of phonons.
\mathcal{C}
is the “collision integral,” which captures in particular the
scattering of phonons with other degrees of freedom (
Q
fields whose coupling to
the phonons is given by
H^{\prime}
in Eq. (
I
)). In turn, using Born’s approximation,
we have the following perturbative expansion of the scattering matrix:

T_{\mathtt{i\rightarrow f}}=T_{\mathtt{fi}}=\langle\mathtt{f}|H^{\prime}|\mathtt{i}\rangle+\sum_{\mathtt{n}}\frac{\langle\mathtt{f}|H^{\prime}|\mathtt{n}\rangle\langle\mathtt{n}|H^{\prime}|\mathtt{i}\rangle}{E_{\mathtt{i}}-E_{\mathtt{n}}+i\eta}+\cdots,

(7)

where the
|\mathtt{i},\mathtt{f},\mathtt{n}\rangle
states are
product states in the
Q
(index
s
) and phonon (index
p
) Hilbert space,

|\mathtt{g}\rangle=|g_{s}\rangle|g_{p}\rangle
for

g=i,f,n
, and
E_{\mathtt{g}}
is the energy of the unperturbed
Hamiltonians of the
Q
and phonons in state
{\mathtt{g}}
.
\eta\rightarrow 0^{+}

is a small regularization parameter. The expression
Eq. (
7
) can be derived from time-dependent perturbation (scattering)
theory, in which
\eta
captures causality and the regularizability of

1/(E_{\mathtt{i}}-E_{\mathtt{n}})
in the case of a continuous energy
spectrum, appropriate for scattering (unbounded) states which we are
interested in
Landau and Lifshitz 2013
.

The rate of transitions
from state
\mathtt{i}
to state
\mathtt{f}
is obtained using Fermi’s golden rule,

\Gamma_{\mathtt{i\rightarrow f}}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\,|T_{\mathtt{i\rightarrow f}}|^{2}\delta(E_{\mathtt{i}}-E_{\mathtt{f}}).

(8)

Note that
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i\rightarrow f}}
is a transition rate in
the full combined phonon-
Q
system. This in turn determines the
collision integral through the master equation

\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{i_{p},f_{p}}\tilde{\Gamma}_{i_{p}\rightarrow f_{p}}\left(N_{n\mathbf{k}}(f_{p})-N_{n\mathbf{k}}(i_{p})\right)p_{i_{p}},

(9)

where
p_{i_{p}}=\sum_{i_{s}}p_{\mathtt{i}}
, where
p_{\mathtt{i}}=\frac{1}{Z}e^{-\beta E_{\mathtt{i}}}
is
the probability to find the system in state
\mathtt{i}
, and

Z
is the partition function of the two subsystems. Here

\tilde{\Gamma}_{i_{p}\rightarrow f_{p}}=\sum_{i_{s}f_{s}}\;\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\,p_{i_{s}}

(10)

is the transition rate between just phonon states, with

p_{i_{s}}=\frac{1}{Z_{s}}e^{-\beta E_{i_{s}}}
.

II.2
Discussion

The above approach is “semiclassical” in two respects. First, it
ultimately treats phonons as quasiparticles within a Boltzmann
equation. This is justified whenever the scattering rate is small
compared to the energy of the particles.
Second, we use the Fermi’s
golden rule relation, Eq. (
8
), to determine the scattering
rates. This approximation leads to slight differences from an exact
calculation of the quantum rates, but preserves all symmetries and
physical processes, and we expect it to capture all the key features
of a fully quantum approach. We proceed with the T-matrix approach here which has the
advantage of (relative) physical transparency, as every effect can be
directly identified with a scattering process.

One can understand the need for effects beyond the first
Born approximation entirely through the symmetries of the T-matrix.
Specifically, since the time reversal (TR) operator is anti-unitary,
and requires complex conjugation, one can see from
Eq. (
7
) that under time reversal,
{\rm TR}:T\mapsto T^{\dagger}

(
\eta\rightarrow-\eta
under complex conjugation). Since TR
invariance is sufficient to enforce a vanishing Hall effect, the hermiticity
of
T
is enough to guarantee a vanishing Hall effect. From
Eq. (
7
),
T
is indeed always hermitian within the first
Born approximation, because
H^{\prime}
itself must be hermitian.

Finally, we note that we are focusing on collisional effects, i.e. on

real
transitions induced by interactions, rather than Berry phase
contributions, which arise from entirely virtual transitions and
manifest as modifications to the semiclassical equations of motion for
phonons, e.g. an anomalous velocity. Formally, real transitions are
captured within the collision integral on the right hand side of the
Boltzmann equation
Mori et al. 2014
, while Berry phase contributions enter
the left hand side and in the definition of the currents. For
phonons, our focus on collisions is justified by strong phase space
constraints on the Berry curvature effects which are typical to
acoustic bosonic modes. Specifically, as shown in
Ref.
Qin et al. 2012
, the Berry phase contributions are described
by an emergent vector potential which at small momenta must by
symmetry be at least second order in gradients, making it a formally
“irrelevant” perturbation to the phonon Lagrangian, and strongly
suppressing its effects at low temperature
Ye et al. 2021
.

III
Formal expressions for the thermal conductivity

III.1
Formal expressions

To solve Eq. (
6
), we expand
\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}=N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\delta\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}
around the
equilibrium distribution
N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}}
, which solves Boltzmann’s equation at
\boldsymbol{\nabla}T=\mathbf{0}
,
keep terms up to linear order in
\delta\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}
in
the collision integral and for convenience separate the diagonal
D_{n\mathbf{k}}
and
off-diagonal
M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

parts, i.e. we write the collision integral

\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\big(-\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}D_{n\mathbf{k}}+M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\big)\delta\overline{N}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+O(\delta\overline{N}^{2}),

(11)

where by definition
M_{n\mathbf{k},n\mathbf{k}}=0
. The equation
\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}[\{N^{\rm eq}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\}]=0
—i.e. the collision integral is zero in
equilibrium—should be considered the
definition
of the
equilibrium densities
\{N^{\rm eq}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\}
of the interacting phonons (see Appendix
C.3
).

Using Fourier’s law,

\mathbf{j}=-\boldsymbol{\kappa}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}T=V^{-1}\sum_{n\mathbf{k}}\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}\boldsymbol{v}_{n\mathbf{k}}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}},

(12)

and formally inverting the collision integral leads to the following
expressions for the longitudinal
\kappa_{L}^{\mu\mu}
, and Hall
(antisymmetric)
\kappa_{H}^{\mu\nu}=(\kappa^{\mu\nu}-\kappa^{\nu\mu})/2
conductivities (along the
\mu
direction and in the
\mu\nu
plane, respectively):

\kappa_{L/H}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{k_{B}T^{2}}\frac{1}{V}\sum_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}K^{L/H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}J^{\nu}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

(13)

where
\nu=\mu
for
\kappa_{L}
. Assuming
\sum_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}M_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\ll D_{n\mathbf{k}}
, one can effectively invert the
collision integral to obtain the kernels

\displaystyle K^{L}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}}{D_{n\mathbf{k}}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\frac{e^{\beta\hbar(\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})/2}}{2D_{n\mathbf{k}}D_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\left(\frac{\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2)}{\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+(n\mathbf{k}\leftrightarrow n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\right),

(14)

\displaystyle K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\beta\hbar(\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})/2}}{2D_{n\mathbf{k}}D_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\left(\frac{\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2)}{\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-(n\mathbf{k}\leftrightarrow n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\right).

(15)

Here we identified the equilibrium phonon current

J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}=N_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\rm eq}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}v^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}
,
and made the “standard” approximation

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\mathbf{r}}\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}\approx\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\mathbf{r}}N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}}
, and looked for a
stationary solution (
\partial_{t}\overline{N}=0
) to Boltzmann’s
equation. While the sign of
\kappa_{H}
depends on the details of the
system (see later), the second law of thermodynamics imposes

\kappa_{L}>0
.
Considering
Eq. (
14
), we therefore expect
D_{n\mathbf{k}}>0
.

Clearly, only contributions to
K^{L/H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

which are symmetric (resp. antisymmetric) in exchanging

(n\mathbf{k}\leftrightarrow n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
contribute to
\kappa_{L}

(resp.
\kappa_{H}
). The special case of the term diagonal in

n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
, being symmetric, does not contribute to
the Hall conductivity. Below we will isolate the correlation functions
of the
Q
operators which give anti-symmetric (in

n\mathbf{k}\leftrightarrow n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
) contributions to

\frac{\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2)}{\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, and hence contribute to
\kappa_{H}
.
These correspond to scattering processes which violate detailed
balance.

III.2
Model

To describe
the interaction between the phonons and another degree of freedom, we
introduce general coupling terms between phonon annihilation
(creation)
operators
a^{(\dagger)}_{n\mathbf{k}}
and general, for now
unspecified, fields
Q^{\{q_{j}\}}_{\{n_{j},\mathbf{k}_{j}\}}
which
are operators acting in their own Hilbert
space. In what follows we only
consider the first two terms of the expansion with respect to phonon
operators (see also Eq. (
I
)), i.e. we write the interaction hamiltonian as

H^{\prime}=H^{\prime}_{[1]}+H^{\prime}_{[2]}
, where

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{[1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\mathbf{k}}\sum_{q=\pm}a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}},

(16)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{[2]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{n\mathbf{k}\neq n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}=\pm}a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}a_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

and in the following, we consider Eq. (
16
) as a perturbative
expansion with respect to a small parameter
{\lambda}
, such that
formally
Q_{n\mathbf{k}}\sim{\lambda},Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sim{\lambda}^{2}
, etc. Note we consider generalizations of this
model in Appendix
D
.

In the above expression we used
a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{+}\equiv a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}
and
a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{-}\equiv a_{n\mathbf{k}}
. The hermiticity
of
H^{\prime}
imposes
Q_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}^{+}\equiv Q_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}^{\dagger}
and

Q_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}^{-}\equiv Q_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}
, and for many-phonon
terms, we have

Q^{-q_{1},...,-q_{M}}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}}=(Q^{q_{1}...q_{M}}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}})^{\dagger}
. The
single-phonon interaction terms, which may physically be seen as single-phonon scattering off the

Q
degrees of freedom, corresponds in particular to a coupling
of the
Q
operators to the strain tensor
\mathcal{E}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{r})
,

\mathcal{E}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{i\hbar^{1/2}}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}n}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\frac{\left(k^{\alpha}\varepsilon^{\beta}_{\mathbf{k}n}+k^{\beta}\varepsilon^{\alpha}_{\mathbf{k}n}\right)}{\sqrt{2M_{\rm uc}\omega_{\mathbf{k}n}}}\left(a_{\mathbf{k}n}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}+a_{-\mathbf{k}n}^{\dagger}\right),

(17)

where
M_{\rm uc}
is the unit cell mass and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n\mathbf{k}}
is the
polarization vector of the
|n\mathbf{k}\rangle
phonon.
The two-phonon terms capture quadratic coupling of the lattice
displacements to the electrons/spins, as is often considered for
example in treatments of Raman scattering

Sheng et al. 2006
;
Kagan and Maksimov 2008
. A
priori, the quadratic terms are much smaller than the linear
ones, but the former may be important if they give rise to distinct
effects or contribute at a lower order in perturbation theory
than the linear ones.

III.3
Scattering rates

III.3.1
T-matrix elements

The transition matrix elements are

T_{\mathtt{fi}}=\sum_{l}T_{\mathtt{fi}}^{[l_{1},...]}
(the
l_{i}

represent which
H_{[l_{i}]}
appear successively in
T
, so that the
number of
l_{i}
appearing in
T_{\mathtt{fi}}^{[l_{1},...]}
is the order of the Born approximation used
for that term), where

\displaystyle T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\mathbf{k}q}\sqrt{N^{i}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\tfrac{1+q}{2}}~\langle f_{s}|Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}|i_{s}\rangle~\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\longrightarrow}{f_{p}}),

(18)

\displaystyle T^{[2]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}q^{\prime}}\sqrt{N_{n\mathbf{k}}^{i}+\tfrac{1+q}{2}}\sqrt{N_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{i}+\tfrac{1+q^{\prime}}{2}}\langle f_{s}|Q_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{qq^{\prime}}|i_{s}\rangle~\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\underset{q^{\prime}\cdot n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}{f_{p}}),

(19)

\displaystyle T^{[1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}\sqrt{N^{i}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\tfrac{1+q}{2}}\sqrt{N^{f}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1-q^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{m_{s}}\frac{\langle f_{s}|Q^{q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|m_{s}\rangle\langle m_{s}|Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}|i_{s}\rangle}{E_{i_{s}}-E_{m_{s}}-q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+i\eta}~\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\underset{q^{\prime}\cdot n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}{f_{p}}),

(20)

and
T^{[1,2]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}
and

T^{[1,1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}
are given in
Appendices
C.4
(Eq. (
204
)) and

C.5
(Eq. (
208
)), respectively. Here,
\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\longrightarrow}{f_{p}})
(resp.
\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\underset{q^{\prime}\cdot n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}{f_{p}})
) is a large product of delta functions which enforce

N^{f}_{n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}=N^{i}_{n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}

\forall n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}\neq n\mathbf{k}
(resp.
\forall n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}\neq(n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
), and

N^{f}_{n\mathbf{k}}=N^{i}_{n\mathbf{k}}+q
(resp.
N^{f}_{n\mathbf{k}}=N^{i}_{n\mathbf{k}}+q,N^{f}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=N^{i}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+q^{\prime}
). Note that the cases where

n\mathbf{k}=n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
require a
formal correction. However, at any given order in the
{\lambda}
expansion, such terms are smaller than all others
by a factor
1/N_{\rm uc}
, where
N_{\rm uc}
is the
number of unit cells, and therefore vanish in the
thermodynamic limit. In what follows we thus use
\sum_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
\sum_{n\mathbf{k}\neq n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

exchangeably, unless we specify
otherwise.

The scattering rate as given by Eq. (
8
), involves the squares of
the elements of the total transition matrix (see Appendices
C.4
,

C.5
for computational details).
Its full expression to perturbative order
\lambda^{4}
is

\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}=\Gamma^{\texttt{SC}}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}+\Gamma^{\texttt{Q}1}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}+\Gamma^{\texttt{Q}2}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}},

(21)

where

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\Gamma^{\texttt{SC}}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}&;&\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\texttt{Q}2}&;&\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\texttt{Q}1}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\delta(E_{\mathtt{i}}-E_{\mathtt{f}})\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\times\begin{bmatrix}|T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}|^{2}+|T^{[1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}|^{2}+|T^{[2]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}|^{2}\quad;\\
2\mathfrak{Re}\left\{(T^{[1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}})^{*}T^{[2]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\right\}\quad;\\
2\mathfrak{Re}\left\{(T^{[1,2]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}})^{*}T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}+(T^{[1,1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}})^{*}T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\right\}\end{bmatrix}.\quad

(22)

This decomposition into three terms is
discussed in Sec.
III.4.3
.

III.3.2
Collision matrix elements

Following Eq. (
9
), the scattering rates
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}

give access to the collision integral, i.e. to
M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
D_{n\mathbf{k}}
.
We decompose the latter as
D_{n\mathbf{k}}=D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(1)}+D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(2)}+\breve{D}_{n\mathbf{k}}
,
where
D^{(1)}
and
D^{(2)}
are obtained in our perturbative expansion at orders

\lambda^{2}
and
\lambda^{4}
, respectively, and
\breve{D}_{n\mathbf{k}}
encompasses contributions
due to other scattering processes as well as higher-order terms of the expansion.
In the following, we also use the “
[l_{i}];[l^{\prime}_{j}]
” superscripts to denote a term
obtained from the product of
T_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{[l_{i}]}
and

T_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{[l^{\prime}_{j}]}
within
|T_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}|^{2}
.
For instance, at order
\lambda^{2}
, we have

D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(1)}=D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{[1];[1]}.

(23)

Details of the derivation are given in Sec.
III.4.1
and
Appendix
C.2
.
At order
\lambda^{4}
, the diagonal and off-diagonal contributions to the collision integral
take the forms

D^{(2)}_{n\mathbf{k}}=-\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}}\sum_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{qq^{\prime}}q\left(N^{\rm eq}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\tfrac{q^{\prime}+1}{2}\right)\left[\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{qq^{\prime}}\right],

(24)

and

M_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}=\pm}q\left(N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\tfrac{q+1}{2}\right)\left[\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{qq^{\prime}}\right],

(25)

respectively, where
\mathfrak{W}^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is an
off-diagonal scattering rate which involves two different
phonon states
|n\mathbf{k}\rangle
and
|n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\rangle
. More precisely,

\mathfrak{W}^{+,+}
(resp.
\mathfrak{W}^{-,-}
) corresponds to scattering processes where two
phonons are emitted (resp. absorbed), and
\mathfrak{W}^{+,-},\mathfrak{W}^{-,+}
to processes where one phonon is emitted and one is
absorbed.
D_{n\mathbf{k}}
is
the diagonal scattering rate, i.e. it is associated with
variations in
\delta\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}
only.

We will now decompose the
\mathfrak{W}^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
scattering
rates into

\mathfrak{W}^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\oplus,qq^{\prime}}+\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\ominus,qq^{\prime}},

(26)

where
\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus/\ominus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
satisfy detailed (
\sigma=1
) or
“anti-detailed” (
\sigma=-1
) balance equations

\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}=\sigma~e^{-\beta(q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}~\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\sigma,-q-q^{\prime}},\quad\sigma=\pm\,{\rm or}\,\oplus,\ominus.

(27)

Physically, Eq. (
27
) expresses “microscopic” thermodynamic
equilibrium between the process which takes

\{N_{n\mathbf{k}}\rightarrow N_{n\mathbf{k}}+q,N_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\rightarrow N_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+q^{\prime}\}
to the “conjugate”
process taking
\{N_{n\mathbf{k}}\rightarrow N_{n\mathbf{k}}-q,N_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\rightarrow N_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-q^{\prime}\}
, with
q,q^{\prime}=\pm 1
, leaving

N_{n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}
unchanged for

n^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}\notin\{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\}
.
Note
that this is different from time-reversal symmetry which provides a
relation between the processes acting on
\{|n_{l},\mathbf{k}_{l}\rangle\}

phonons to the
same
processes acting on the

\{|n_{l},-\mathbf{k}_{l}\rangle\}
phonons.

Moreover, since, by construction, the two-phonon scattering rates
satisfy

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,q^{\prime}q}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}n\mathbf{k}},

(28)

the following relations also hold:

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,+-}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}n\mathbf{k}}=\sigma\,e^{\beta(\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}-\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}}^{\prime})}\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,+-}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}.

(29)

Together, these imply that there are only four independent such scattering
rates between the
|n,\mathbf{k}\rangle
and
|n^{\prime},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\rangle
phonons, namely
\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,++}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,+-}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

with
\sigma=\oplus,\ominus
.

As discussed at length, the first Born approximation alone does not
lead to a nonzero thermal Hall effect, neither do those scattering
rates which satisfy detailed balance as the latter imposes thermal equilibrium
between “left” and “right” scattering. We find the kernels
K^{L/H}
defined in Eqs. (
14
,
15
)
in terms of the
\mathfrak{W}
scattering rates:

\displaystyle K_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}}{D_{n\mathbf{k}}}\Bigg(\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\frac{e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}}{2N_{\rm uc}D_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\sum_{q=\pm}e^{\frac{q-1}{2}\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\left\{\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,+,q}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\left(q\coth(\tfrac{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}{2})+\coth(\tfrac{\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{2})\right)-2\,\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,+,q}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\right\}\Bigg),

\displaystyle K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}}{2N_{\rm uc}D_{n\mathbf{k}}D_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\sum_{q=\pm}\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,+,q}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\,e^{\frac{q-1}{2}\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\left(\coth(\tfrac{\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{2})-q\coth(\tfrac{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}{2})\right).

(31)

Incorporating the expression for
D
in the
denominators of
K^{L,H}
provides an
expansion up to
O({\lambda}^{4})
of the latter.
We recover, as mentioned before, that the terms in
\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus}
do
not contribute to
K^{H}
(they satisfy detailed-balance). The
“anti-detailed-balance” relations satisfied by the
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus}

terms do not however prohibit their contribution to
K^{L}
.
See Sec.
IV.2
for a discussion. Inserting
Eq. (
III.3.2
) and Eq. (
31
) into
Eq. (
13
), and after some algebra, one obtains the results
for
\kappa_{\rm L,H}
, respectively:

\displaystyle\kappa_{\rm L}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{k_{B}T^{2}}\frac{1}{V}\Bigg\{\sum_{n\mathbf{k}}\frac{\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}^{2}v^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}v^{\nu}_{n\mathbf{k}}}{4D_{n\mathbf{k}}\sinh^{2}(\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2)}

(32)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}\frac{e^{\beta\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2}}{D_{n\mathbf{k}}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}}\sum_{q=\pm}e^{\beta q\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2}e^{\beta\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2}\Big\{\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\ominus,+q}{\rm coth}(\beta\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)-q\mathfrak{W}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\oplus,+q}\Big\}\Bigg)\frac{e^{\beta\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2}}{D_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}J^{\nu}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\Bigg\},

where the first term is the leading-order contribution
and with
J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}=N_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\rm eq}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}v^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}
, and

\kappa_{\rm H}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\hbar^{2}}{k_{B}T^{2}}\frac{1}{V}\sum_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}\frac{e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2}}{D_{n\mathbf{k}}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}}\sum_{q=\pm}\frac{\left(e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}-e^{q\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\right)\,\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,+,q}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{4\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}/2)\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2)}\Bigg)\frac{e^{\beta\hbar\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}/2}}{D_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}J^{\nu}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}.

(33)

III.4
The collision integral as correlation functions

III.4.1
Terms at
O(\lambda^{2})

The diagonal scattering rate
D^{(1)}_{n\mathbf{k}}
, obtained by
inserting
T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}
from Eq. (
18
) into Eqs. (
8
-
10
),
may now be cast into the form of a correlation function of
Q
operators.
To do so, we first enforce the energy conservation

\delta(E_{\mathtt{f}}-E_{\mathtt{i}})
by writing the latter
as a time integral, i.e. use
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm d}te^{i\omega t}=2\pi\delta(\omega)
; we then identify
A(t)=e^{+iHt}Ae^{-iHt}
and use the
identity
1=\sum_{f_{s}}|f_{s}\rangle\langle f_{s}|
.
Taking the
Q
s in the initial state to be in thermal equilibrium

p_{i_{s}}=Z_{s}^{-1}e^{-\beta E_{i_{s}}}
, summing over
|i_{s}\rangle
, identifying
\langle A\rangle_{\beta}=Z^{-1}\text{Tr}(e^{-\beta H}A)
, summing over final phononic states
f_{p}
and
taking the average over initial phononic states
i_{p}
, we obtain the

|T_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{[1]}|^{2}
contribution of
\Gamma^{\mathtt{SC}}
,

D^{(1)}_{n\mathbf{k}}=-\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\int dte^{-i\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}t}\left\langle[Q^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{n\mathbf{k}}(t),Q^{\dagger}_{n\mathbf{k}}(0)]\right\rangle_{\beta}.

(34)

We now apply the same method to higher orders of the perturbative expansion.

III.4.2
Terms at
O(\lambda^{4})

We use the following
time integral representation for the denominators appearing at second
and higher Born orders (using a regularized
definition of the sign function, i.e.
\underset{\eta\rightarrow 0}{\lim}{\rm sign}(t)e^{-\eta|t|}\rightarrow{\rm sign}(t)
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x\pm i\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm PP}\frac{1}{x}\mp i\pi\delta(x)

(35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2i}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\text{d}t_{1}e^{it_{1}x}\left(\text{sign}(t_{1})\pm 1\right).

Using Eqs. (
19
,
20
) and Eq. (
III.3.1
), we find the explicit
expressions for the other semiclassical (
\Gamma^{\mathtt{SC}}
) scattering rates as correlation functions of the

Q
operators,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[2];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\hbar^{4}}~\fint_{t}\qquad\left\langle Q^{-q,-q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t)Q^{q,q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(0)\right\rangle,

(36)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\hbar^{4}}~N_{\rm uc}~\mathfrak{Re}~\fint_{t,t_{1},t_{2}}\left\langle\llbracket Q^{-q}_{n\mathbf{k}}(-t-t_{2}),Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t+t_{2})\rrbracket\{Q^{q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t_{1}),Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}(t_{1})\}\right\rangle,

(37)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hbar^{4}}~N_{\rm uc}~\fint_{t,t_{1},t_{2}}\left\langle\{\cdot,\cdot\}\{\cdot,\cdot\}-\llbracket\cdot,\cdot\rrbracket\llbracket\cdot,\cdot\rrbracket\right\rangle,

(38)

where we use the shorthand notation

\llbracket A(t_{a}),B(t_{b})\rrbracket={\rm sign}(t_{b}-t_{a})[A(t_{a}),B(t_{b})],

(39)

and
\fint_{t,\{t_{j}\}}
,
j=1,..,l
, denotes the set of
1+l

Fourier transforms evaluated once at

\Sigma_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
l
times at

\Delta_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, i.e.
\fint_{t,\{t_{j}\}}=\int\text{d}t\text{d}t_{1}..\text{d}t_{l}e^{i\Sigma_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}t}e^{i\Delta_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}(t_{1}+..+t_{l})}
. The symbols
\cdot
must
be replaced by the same set of operators as the expression from the
above. The commutators and
anticommutators ultimately capture antisymmetrization and
symmetrization over the
n\mathbf{k}q\leftrightarrow n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}

indices. We provide expressions for

\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
,

\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
(from

\Gamma^{\mathtt{Q2}}
),

\mathfrak{W}^{[1,2];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and

\mathfrak{W}^{[1,1,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
(from

\Gamma^{\mathtt{Q1}}
) in
Appendices
C.4.4
,
C.5
,
Eqs. (
202
,
203
,
205
,
210
), respectively.

III.4.3
Scattering channels and conserving approximation

The above terms capture all contributions to the collision
integral arising from the Born expansion of the transition amplitude,
up to perturbative order
\lambda^{4}
.
This gives, correspondingly, physical processes in the collision
integral which contribute up to
O(\lambda^{4})
.

In Eq. (
21
), while
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{SC}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{Q}2}
are “two-phonon” terms, the contribution from
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{Q}1}
is
a “one-phonon” term, i.e. one where the initial
\mathtt{i}
and final
\mathtt{f}

states differ by only one phonon
|n\mathbf{k}\rangle
.
Physically, this contributes to processes
which create or annihilate a single phonon, in contrast with the

O(\lambda^{4})
processes described so far, which create/annihilate
two phonons with different quantum numbers. Because the single
phonon process is physically distinct from the two-phonon ones, we
expect that it is independent from the latter in the sense that the
set of all the
O(\lambda^{4})
single-phonon processes satisfies
independently
all
physical constraints such as symmetries and conservation laws.
Hence omitting these contributions is a “conserving
approximation” in the traditional sense
Baym and Kadanoff 1961
,
and we will proceed with
this omission for the most part in the following. We however
include formal expressions for these terms in the appendices.

The remaining contributions in Eq. (
21
) are “two-phonon”
terms, i.e. terms in which the initial
\mathtt{i}
and final
\mathtt{f}
states differ by two
phonons
|n\mathbf{k}\rangle,|n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\rangle
. The two-phonon,

O(\lambda^{4})
, contributions to the
\mathfrak{W}
scattering rates thus read

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

(40)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[2];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}.

Another physical distinction between the contributions in
Eq. (
21
) can be
made according to the “quantum” or semiclassical,
nature of the terms. The one-phonon
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{Q}1}
and two-phonon
\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{Q}2}
terms in Eq. (
21
) are “quantum” in
the sense that the physical process corresponding to each contribution therein
is an interference term between
distinct
scattering channels.
In particular, in a “quantum” term, the number of scattering events
in the two channels are different. On the contrary, each contribution in

\Gamma_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}^{\mathtt{SC}}
is the probability amplitude
of one given scattering channel, corresponding physically to the probability amplitude
of a given scattering process, and in this respect is truly semiclassical.
As a semiclassical approximation, we will neglect “quantum” contributions in the following;
formal expressions for these terms are nonetheless included in the appendices.
The only “semiclassical” contributions, up to
O({\lambda}^{4})
, to the collision integral are
from the scattering rates shown in Eq. (
36
).

Upon applying our results to the case of a staggered antiferromagnet
in Sec.
V
, we focus on the lowest-order contributions
to
K^{L}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
,
which come from
D^{(1)}_{n\mathbf{k}}
and
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, respectively.
Therefore, in Sec.
V
, we consider only the lowest-order semiclassical contributions

D_{n\mathbf{k}}\approx D^{(1)}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\breve{D}_{n\mathbf{k}}
and

\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\approx\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
.

III.4.4
Physical interpretation

To leading order, the longitudinal conductivity is controlled by
the diagonal scattering rate, whose main contribution occurs at
order
\lambda^{2}
. The latter is given as the first term in Eq. (
2
),
and is shown explicitly in Eq. (
34
). It is related to the
Fourier transform of the commutator of two

Q_{n\mathbf{k}}
operators at unequal times. The commutator structure identifies the phonon scattering rate

D^{(1)}_{n\mathbf{k}}
with the spectral function of the
Q_{n\mathbf{k}}
field at energy
\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
, i.e. it captures
the proportion of the energy density contained in the
Q_{n\mathbf{k}}
field located at

\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
, as expected from (lowest-order) linear response

Bruus and Flensberg 2004
;
Gangadharaiah et al. 2010
.

As mentioned above, the first Born order transition matrices are
hermitian. At second Born’s order, the advanced/retarded Green’s
function,
1/(E_{\mathtt{i}/\mathtt{f}}-E_{\mathtt{m}}\pm i\eta)
, appearing in
T_{\mathtt{i}\mapsto\mathtt{f}}
, splits into
on-shell and off-shell contributions, so that the scattering rate

\propto|T_{\mathtt{i}\mapsto\mathtt{f}}|^{2}
then
involves the product of two on-shell or two-offshell contributions, as
well as the products of one on-shell and one off-shell one. Because of complex conjugation of one
term upon taking the square modulus of the
T
matrix, the scattering rates which involve either two
on-shell or two off-shell contributions are blind to the sign of

\pm i\eta
, i.e. to the advanced or retarded nature of
the process, and enforce a detailed-balance relation,
Eq. (
27
) with
\sigma=\oplus
. Therefore,
the only scattering rates which can contribute to the Hall
conductivity are those involving one on-shell
(imaginary part) and one off-shell (real part) scattering event, which
translates here into the product of a commutator and an
anticommutator, Eq. (
37
).

IV
Relations and symmetries

In this section, we explore in more detail some physical relations
verified by the scattering rates defined above, and their possible
consequences on the longitudinal and Hall conductivities.

IV.1
Time-reversal symmetry: reversal of the momenta

Q
operators

\wideparen{Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}}=Q^{q}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}

\wideparen{Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}=Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n-\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

scattering rates

D^{(1)}_{n,\mathbf{k}}=D^{(1)}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\sigma\,\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}_{n-\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

conjugate process

\frac{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}({\rm mr}[S])}{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}(S)}=e^{-\delta E_{s\rightarrow p}^{(S)}}\frac{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}({\rm pc}[S])}{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}(S)}=\sigma

kernels

K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=-K^{H}_{n-\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

conductivities

\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{H}=\boldsymbol{0}

Table 1
:
Relations which hold true in the presence of time-reversal
symmetry
.
The phonon operator relation
\wideparen{a^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}}=a^{q}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}
holds true even when no time-reversal
symmetry is present.
See text for definitions and justifications.

We investigate the implications of time-reversal (TR) invariance on
our results. In particular, we check explicitly that the Hall
conductivity vanishes in a TR-symmetric system. It is important to note that, in a time-reversal
invariant system, the scattering rates are a priori not time-reversal
invariant themselves.

We denote with
\wideparen{Q}
and
|\wideparen{\mathtt{n}}\rangle

the time-reversed of operator

Q
and of state
|{\mathtt{n}}\rangle
, respectively. Then, because
of the antiunitarity of the time-reversal operator, for any states

\mathtt{n,m}
and any operator
Q
, we have

\langle\wideparen{\mathtt{n}}|Q^{\dagger}|\wideparen{\mathtt{m}}\rangle=\langle\mathtt{m}|\wideparen{Q}|\mathtt{n}\rangle
. Moreover, it is possible to choose a polarization index

n
invariant under TR, whence
\wideparen{a^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}}=a^{q}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}
.

Let us now consider what happens in a time-reversal-invariant
system. In that case, the hamiltonian
H^{\prime}_{[1]}=\sum_{n\mathbf{k}q}Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}a^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}
must be TR-invariant, so that

\wideparen{Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}}=Q^{q}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}
. Similarly,
TR-invariance of
H^{\prime}_{[2]}
(defined in Eq. (
16
)) entails

\wideparen{Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}=Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n-\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
.

IV.1.1
Consequences for the scattering rates.

Following the same steps as those sketched in Sec.
III.4.1
, and using the fact that

E_{\wideparen{\mathtt{m}}}=E_{\mathtt{m}}
for any state
\mathtt{m}
of a TR-symmetric system,
we can show explicitly that,
in a time-reversal-invariant
system
, the following relations for the scattering rates exist:

\displaystyle D^{(1)}_{n,\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D^{(1)}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}

(41)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma\,\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}_{n-\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}.

(42)

The
\sigma
sign in the second relation
can be understood as arising from two facts:
(1)
schematically,

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}\sim\frac{1}{E_{\mathtt{f}}-E_{\mathtt{m}}+i\eta}\frac{1}{E_{\mathtt{i}}-E_{\mathtt{m}}-i\eta}+\sigma\,\rm h.c.
—which is reflected in the fact that
\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus}
(resp.
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus}
)
expressed as an integral, Eqs. (
36
–
38
), contains an even (resp. odd) number of
sign functions—
and
(2)
an effect of time-reversal on the
T
-matrix is to exchange denominators

\frac{1}{E_{\mathtt{f}}-E_{\mathtt{m}}+i\eta}\rightarrow\frac{1}{E_{\wideparen{\mathtt{f}}}-E_{\wideparen{\mathtt{m}}}-i\eta}

(see Sec.
III.4.4
for an interpretation of the
+i\eta
regularization).

IV.1.2
Relation to detailed balance.

The decomposition of the scattering rate
\mathfrak{W}^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\sum_{\sigma}\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
into odd
and even terms under the “conjugation” (in the
sense of detailed balance, i.e. thermodynamic equilibrium) of the
associated scattering processes, is also that of its decomposition
into terms, odd and even under the inversion of momentum, in the
presence of time-reversal symmetry. Indeed, if a scattering process

S=\big(\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\underset{q^{\prime}\cdot n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}\big)
transfers an energy

\delta E_{s\rightarrow p}^{(S)}=q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
to the phonon system,
(anti-)detailed balance with the “conjugate process”
{\rm pc}[S]=\big(\overset{-q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\underset{-q^{\prime}\cdot n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}\big)
reads

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}({\rm pc}[S])=\sigma e^{\delta E_{s\rightarrow p}^{(S)}}\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}(S)
.
Meanwhile, in the presence of time-reversal symmetry, the momentum-reversal symmetry reads

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}({\rm mr}[S])=\sigma\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}(S)
, for the “momentum-reversed” process

{\rm mr}[S]=\big(\overset{q\cdot n-\mathbf{k}}{\underset{q^{\prime}\cdot n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}\big)
.
In other words, in a time-reversal
invariant system, the scattering rate associated with the process
“conjugate” of a given process
S
coincides (up to a Boltzmann weight) with that of its
momentum-reversed one:

\frac{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}({\rm mr}[S])}{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}(S)}=e^{-\delta E_{s\rightarrow p}^{(S)}}\frac{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}({\rm pc}[S])}{\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma}(S)}=\sigma.

(43)

Hence, while
\sigma
was defined as signature of the behavior
of the scattering rates under “process conjugation,” it
is
also
that of momentum
reversal in a time-reversal invariant system.
1
1

1

Notice, however, that this coincidence does not survive the breaking of time reversal symmetry.

IV.1.3
Consequences for the kernels.

How is this reflected in the kernels
K^{L},K^{H}
?
Because the relation

\boldsymbol{v}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\mathbf{k}}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}=-\boldsymbol{v}_{n,-\mathbf{k}}
holds,
only that component of
K^{L/H}
which is even upon reversal
of the momenta,
\mathbf{k}^{(}{}^{\prime}{}^{)}\leftrightarrow-\mathbf{k}^{(}{}^{\prime}{}^{)}
, has a non-vanishing contribution
to the sum Eq. (
13
). A first consequence of this is that, in a TR-invariant system, the identity

K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=-K^{H}_{n-\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

(44)

entails
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{H}=\boldsymbol{0}
– as per Onsager’s reciprocity relations
stating that
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{H}
is TR-odd. Note that
K^{L}
in Eq. (
III.3.2
) involves both
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus}
and
\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus}
. Therefore, there is no analog to Eq. (
44
) for
K^{L}
. However, in a TR-invariant system, the
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus}
term
in
K^{L}
does not contribute to
\kappa_{L}
– this is consistent with the Onsager-Casimir relations
which state that
\kappa_{L}
is TR-even.

This indeed reflects the previous discussion as follows: when time reversal is preserved,
TR-even
\kappa_{L}
gets contributions solely from
“detailed-balance-even” and TR-even
\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus}
. On the other hand
TR-odd
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{H}
gets contributions solely from
“detailed-balance-odd” and TR-odd
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus}
. Since the system
is actually TR-even,
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{H}
vanishes.

IV.2
Point-group symmetries

Here we provide some sufficient (but non-necessary) conditions on
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
under which the Hall conductivity vanishes.

IV.2.1
Curie relations

From Fourier’s law
j^{\mu}=-\kappa^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}T
, the Curie
and Onsager relations provide general constraints on the
\kappa^{\mu\nu}
coefficients, and in turn on its Hall component
\kappa_{H}^{\mu\nu}
. In Table
2
, we look at the
D_{4h}=D_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
point group—the largest tetragonal point group— with the associated axes aligned with the orthogonal basis
(\mu,\nu,\rho)
(
\mu\nu
is the basal plane and
\rho
the transverse direction). We can see that if the system is invariant under any one of the transformations
g\in D_{4h}
which are odd under the
A_{2g}
representation (i.e.
C^{\prime}_{2}
,
C^{\prime\prime}_{2}
,
\sigma_{v}
,
\sigma_{d}
), the Hall conductivity must vanish.

:
Elements of the
D_{4h}
point group aligned along the

(\mu,\nu,\rho)
basis (with
\mu\nu
the basal plane), their characters in
the
A_{2g}
irrep (also labeled
\Gamma_{2}^{+}
), and transformations of
\mu,\nu,\rho,\kappa^{\mu\nu},\kappa_{H}^{\mu\nu}
. The

\kappa^{\mu\nu}
and
\kappa_{H}^{\mu\nu}
hold true when the system is invariant under the corresponding

D_{4h}
operation (aligned with the
\mu\nu\rho
basis). The last line is the “category” (cat) to which the operation belongs, as defined in Sec.
IV.2.2
.

is the

axis;

,

and

are
\pi
rotations around the

\rho
axis,

or

axes, and in-plane directions bisecting the
\mu,\nu
axes, respectively;

S_{4}
are
\pi/2
rotations around the
\rho
axis followed by a reflection
through the basal
\mu\nu
plane;
\sigma_{h}
,
\sigma_{v}

and
\sigma_{d}
are reflections through the
\mu\nu
plane (
\sigma_{h}
), through a
plane containing
\mu
or
\nu
and the
\rho
direction (
\sigma_{v}
), and
through a plane containing the
\rho
direction and one bissecting the

\mu,\nu
directions (
\sigma_{d}
), respectively.

IV.2.2
Symmetry relations on
K^{H}

We now turn to relations specific to the scattering situation, i.e. we analyze under which conditions on
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
it befalls that
\kappa^{\mu\nu}_{H}=0
. We start with the expression of

\kappa^{\mu\nu}_{H}
as a momentum integral, Eq. (
13
), i.e.
\kappa_{H}^{\mu\nu}\propto\sum_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}J_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\mu}J_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{\nu}K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and recall
J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}=N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}\partial_{k^{\mu}}\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
.

If the
phonon
system is invariant under a unitary transformation
g
, then
\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
is also invariant under this transformation. In turn only
\mu
in
J^{\mu}_{n\mathbf{k}}
transforms nontrivially under
g
. Therefore:

•

If
the phonon system is invariant under an operation
g\in D_{4h}
which leaves the
\mu,\nu
axes invariant, i.e.
g=C_{2},C_{2}^{\prime},{\rm inv},\sigma_{h},\sigma_{v}
, and
if
one of the two following conditions,
(a)
under
g
the
\mu\nu
product is even (i.e.
g=C_{2},{\rm inv},\sigma_{h}
) and
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is odd,
(b)
under
g
the
\mu\nu
product is odd (i.e.
g=C_{2}^{\prime},\sigma_{v}
) and
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is even, is satisfied,
then
it follows that
\kappa_{\rm H}^{\mu\nu}=0
.

•

Besides, recalling that by construction
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=-K^{H}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}n\mathbf{k}}
,
if
the system is invariant under an operation
g\in D_{4h}
which exchanges the
\mu,\nu
axes, i.e.
g=C_{4},C_{2}^{\prime\prime},S_{4},\sigma_{d}
, and
if
one of the two following conditions,
(c)
under
g
the
\mu\nu
product is even (i.e.
g=C_{2}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{d}
) and
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is even,
(d)
under
g
the
\mu\nu
product is odd (i.e.
g=C_{4},S_{4}
) and
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
is odd, is satisfied,
then
it follows that
\kappa_{\rm H}^{\mu\nu}=0
.

In terms of the behavior of
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, this analysis reduces to:
if

g\in D_{4h}
is a symmetry of the phonon system, and
if

g:K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mapsto-\chi_{A_{2g}}(g)K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, where
\chi_{A_{2g}}(g)
is the character of
g
in the
A_{2g}
representation of the
D_{4h}
point group,
then

\kappa_{H}^{\mu\nu}=0
. We emphasize that this analysis holds
if
the transformation
g
is a symmetry of the phonon system, and whether or not
g
is a symmetry of the whole system. For example, we will show explicitly in Sec.
V.3.4
that
there are cases where, under TR or
\sigma_{d}
the system is not invariant, but the kernel
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and the phonon system are, and so
\kappa_{H}=0
.

Finally, note that the above analysis goes beyond the general predictions from Onsager, which tell us
that

\kappa_{H}
vanishes
in the presence of some symmetries of the
whole
system, namely
C^{\prime}_{2}
,
C^{\prime\prime}_{2}
,
\sigma_{v}
or
\sigma_{d}
(as well as time-reversal discussed in the previous subsection). Here, not only do we establish relations for the other symmetries in
D_{4h}
(as symmetries of the
phonon subsystem only
), we also show
in which way

\kappa_{H}
vanishes, by inspecting the
behavior of the kernels
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
under those symmetry
transformations. In turn, this may for example allow to gather information
about the
system
— about
K^{H}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
—from the
(non-)cancellation of
\kappa_{H}
.

V
Application to an ordered magnet

We now turn to an application of these general results. There, we keep only the
lowest-order terms in the expressions derived above, as described in Sec.
III.4.4
,
and we consider an interaction hamiltonian density which contains
single-phonon interactions with a field
Q
(this is the first term in
Eq. (
I
)) of the form

H^{\prime}=\sum_{n\mathbf{k}}\left(a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}+a_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}\right),

(45)

as obtained from the simplest case of linear coupling to the strain tensor.

We consider an ordered magnetic system, which we take to be a spin-orbit coupled
Néel antiferromagnet with tetragonal symmetry. For
concreteness, we treat the magnetism as purely two-dimensional,
i.e. the full spin+phonon system is described by a stack of two
dimensional antiferromagnets embedded into the three-dimension solid,
so that in particular, we take, when going from the lattice to the
continuum limit

\sum_{\mathbf{r}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\sum_{z}\int d^{2}x,\quad\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\rightarrow\frac{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{k_{z}}\int d^{2}k,

(46)

where
\mathfrak{a}
is the in-plane lattice spacing.

V.1
Magnon dynamics

V.1.1
Low-energy field-theoretical description

We consider a Néel antiferromagnet with a two-site magnetic unit
cell, more precisely a bipartite lattice of spins such that
the classical ground state is ordered in an antiferromagnetic
configuration, with a local moment
\mu_{0}
oriented in the direction

\mathbf{n}
, i.e.
\mathbf{n}
is the Néel vector which has unit
length in the ordered state at zero field. Within standard
spin-wave theory,
\mu_{0}=S
with
S
the spin value. For concreteness, we will
choose the ordering axis at zero field to be aligned along the
\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{x}
axis (the set
(\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{x},\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{y},\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{z})
is an orthonormal cartesian basis)—the results of this
subsection hold regardless of this choice.

A general low energy spin configuration is described by two continuum
fields: the aforementioned Néel vector
\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{r})

and
a
uniform magnetization
density

\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{r})
, such that

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{r}}=(-1)^{\mathbf{r}}\mu_{0}\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{r})+\mathfrak{a}^{2}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{r}).

(47)

where
(-1)^{\mathbf{r}}
is a sign which alternates between neighboring
sites (recall we are considering a Néel antiferromagnet), and both
continuum fields are assumed to be slowly-varying relative to the
lattice spacing. Here
\mathfrak{a}
is the 2d lattice spacing. We
will assume the non-linear sigma model constraint that the spin length
is fixed to
\mu_{0}
, which implies that

|\mathbf{n}|^{2}+\frac{\mathfrak{a}^{4}}{\mu_{0}^{2}}|\mathbf{m}|^{2}=1,\qquad\mathbf{m}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0.

(48)

The spin wave expansion consists of expanding these fields around the
zero field ordered state, i.e.
\mathbf{n}_{\rm ord}=\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{x},\mathbf{m}_{\rm ord}=\boldsymbol{0}
. To linear order around this state, we take

\mathbf{n}=\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{x}+\boldsymbol{n}
and
\mathbf{m}=\boldsymbol{m}
, where
n_{x}=m_{x}=0
,
leaving the remaining degrees of freedom
n_{y},n_{z},m_{y},m_{z}
.
In terms of the spins, this gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\mathbf{r}}\mu_{0}\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{x}+\sum_{a=y,z}((-1)^{\mathbf{r}}\mu_{0}n_{a}(\mathbf{r})+\mathfrak{a}^{2}m_{a}(\mathbf{r}))\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{a}.

(49)

Because the local moment along the
\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{x}
axis is non-zero, the low
energy fields satisfy the commutation relations

[m_{y}(\mathbf{r}),n_{z}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})]=-[m_{z}(\mathbf{r}),n_{y}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime})]=-i\delta(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
.
The low energy continuum Hamiltonian density for these fields is

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rm NLS}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\rho}{2}\left(|\underline{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}n_{y}|^{2}+|\underline{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}n_{z}|^{2}\right)

(50)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\chi}(m_{y}^{2}+m_{z}^{2})+\sum_{a,b=y,z}\frac{\Gamma_{ab}}{2}n_{a}n_{b},

where
\rho
is the spin stiffness constant,
\chi
is the spin
susceptibility,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}=(\partial_{x},\partial_{y})
denotes the in-plane gradient, and the

\Gamma_{ab}
are anisotropy coefficients which open a
small spin wave gap (see App.
F.3
). For
an approximately Heisenberg system with isotropic exchange constant
J
, we have
within spin wave theory that
\chi^{-1}\approx 4J{\mathfrak{a}}^{2}
,
\rho\approx 2J\mu_{0}^{2}
, while
\Gamma_{ab}
are determined by exchange
anisotropies. The choice to normalize
\boldsymbol{m}
as a density while
keeping
\boldsymbol{n}
dimensionless ensures that
m_{y,z}

fields are just the canonical momenta conjugate to the

n_{z,y}
fields, and hence Eq. (
50
) is just a
Hamiltonian density of two
free scalar boson fields.

The above description is appropriate to describe the ordered phase of
the antiferromagnet, for any value of the spin, provided temperature
is low compared to the Néel temperature and any applied magnetic fields
are small compared to the saturation field. These conditions are
well-satisfied in practice in experiments on many antiferromagnets.
Specifically we will be interested in the case with an applied
magnetic field perpendicular to the axis of the Néel vector
(e.g. along
z
or
y
, given the choice in Eq. (
49
)). In general the field induces
a non-zero uniform magnetization along its direction, e.g. for a

z
-axis field
\langle m_{z}\rangle\neq 0
. Such a
“spin flop” configuration is favorable for an antiferromagnet in a
field.

V.1.2
Symmetry considerations

Two symmetries clarify the calculations and provide physical insight.
The first is the macroscopic time-reversal symmetry of the zero field
state, which is what makes it an
anti
-ferromagnet.
Specifically, the system in zero field is invariant under the
combination of time-reversal symmetry TR and a translation

T
. Under this
operation, we see that the continuum fields transform according to

\mathcal{T}={\rm TR}\times T:\quad m\rightarrow-m,\quad n\rightarrow n.

(51)

The presence of a staggered magnetization (with any orientation) does
not break this symmetry, but a uniform magnetization does. Note that
the
effective
quadratic low energy Hamiltonian,
Eq. (
50
), is invariant under this symmetry. This is
true even at non-zero fields, because the low energy Hamiltonian is
quadratic. Thus effects of time-reversal symmetry breaking will
become evident in terms beyond this form, notably in anharmonic
corrections, and in the spin-lattice coupling itself. Specifically,
we see that time-reversal symmetry will be effectively broken only by
terms involving an odd number of powers of the
m_{a}
fields.

The second
important symmetry is one which may be preserved
not only by the underlying exchange Hamiltonian and crystal structure,
but
also
by the applied field and the spontaneous ordered moments.
In particular, the latter breaks the original translational symmetry
of the square lattice by a single lattice spacing. However, a
symmetry may be retained under such a simultaneous translation composed
with a
C_{2}
spin rotation around the field axis. In the presence of
spin-orbit coupling, generically the spin rotation must be accompanied
by a spatial rotation, and the full combined operation is in fact
nothing but a
C_{2}
rotation about an axis passing through the
mid-point of a bond of the square lattice. This of course requires the
C_{2}
rotation in question to be part of the lattice point group. In our problem, this is true when the field is along
z
or
y
(but not for a general orientation in the
yz
plane).

This odd symmetry is important for simplifying the magnon
interactions. In particular, if the field axis is along
z
, then we
see that
m_{z}
and
n_{y}
are both even under this operation, while

m_{y}
and
n_{z}
are odd under it (and vice versa if the field is along
y
). Note that the fields within a
canonically conjugate pair transform the same way under this symmetry.
We take advantage of these facts in the following.
In particular, only
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{yy}
and
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{zz}

do not vanish a priori, which ensures that the two valleys (
\ell=0,1
) are exactly decoupled.

V.1.3
External magnetic field

At the lowest order, an applied external magnetic field
\mathbf{h}
couples solely to the

\boldsymbol{m}
field; this is already taken into account in Eq. (
50
)
where the
\boldsymbol{m}
fields can acquire a (static) nonzero expectation value
due to the spin alignment with the field.

Meanwhile, at higher orders the magnetic field also couples to the
\boldsymbol{n}
field; the main
contribution comes from the square, isotropic coupling
(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\mathbf{h})^{2}
.
Due to the
C_{2}
symmetry around the field axis (
y
or
z
), and since first-order terms
of the form
h_{a}n_{b}
are forbidden by translational symmetry, this results in an additional
term

\mathcal{H}_{\rm field}=\frac{\chi}{2}\sum_{a=y,z}h_{a}^{2}n_{a}^{2}.

(52)

Note this form is valid only when the field is along the
y
or
z
axis, not at other angles in the
y-z
plane (which would violate the
C_{2}
symmetry).
The prefactor
\chi/2
is fixed to match the results obtained from
microscopic calculations in Ref.
Benfatto and Silva Neto 2006
, and we provide an alternative derivation
in App.
F.3
as well as a more detailed
derivation of the full form of the gap from a microscopic XXZ exchange
model plus a Zeeman coupling to the field in App.
F.3
.

V.1.4
Diagonalization

We proceed to diagonalize Eq. (
50
), supplemented
by Eq. (
52
) following the discussion in Sec.
V.1.3
, by
introducing creation and annihilation operators in the standard way
for free fields. We use the Fourier convention
\phi_{\mathbf{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{V}}\int d{\mathbf{x}}\phi({\mathbf{x}})e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}
for any continuum
field
\phi
, where
V
is the volume of the system. Then

\displaystyle m_{\mathbf{k}}^{y}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\chi\Omega_{{\mathbf{k}},0}}{2}}\left(b_{-{\mathbf{k}},0}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}+b_{{\mathbf{k}},0}^{\dagger}\right),

\displaystyle n_{\mathbf{k}}^{z}

\displaystyle=i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi\Omega_{{\mathbf{k}},0}}}\left(b_{-{\mathbf{k}},0}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}-b_{{\mathbf{k}},0}^{\dagger}\right),

\displaystyle m_{\mathbf{k}}^{z}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\chi\Omega_{{\mathbf{k}},1}}{2}}\left(b_{-{\mathbf{k}},1}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}+b_{{\mathbf{k}},1}^{\dagger}\right),

\displaystyle n_{\mathbf{k}}^{y}

\displaystyle=-i\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi\Omega_{{\mathbf{k}},1}}}\left(b_{-{\mathbf{k}},1}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}-b_{{\mathbf{k}},1}^{\dagger}\right),

(53)

where

\displaystyle\Omega_{{\mathbf{k}},\ell}=\sqrt{v_{{\rm m}}^{2}\underline{\mathbf{k}}^{2}+\Delta_{\ell}^{2}},

(54)

with
v_{\rm m}=\sqrt{\rho/\chi}
. The magnon gaps depend on the applied (transverse) magnetic field
in the form

\Delta_{\ell}=\sqrt{\Gamma_{\ell}/\chi+h_{\ell}^{2}},

(55)

with valley index
\ell=0,1
and where we set
h_{0}=h_{y}
and
h_{1}=h_{z}
.
This reflects the explicit breaking of
O(3)
rotational symmetry of
the order parameter
\boldsymbol{n}
by the transverse field. With these definitions, we
obtain

\displaystyle H_{\rm NLS}+H_{\rm field}=\sum_{\ell}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\Omega_{{\mathbf{k}},\ell}b_{{\mathbf{k}},\ell}^{\dagger}b_{{\mathbf{k}},\ell}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}.

(56)

The
b,b^{\dagger}
fields with index
\ell=0
have
opposite
C_{2}
eigenvalue to those with
\ell=1
. This guarantees
that all terms preserving
C_{2}
symmetry must conserve the two boson
flavors modulo 2.

V.2
Formal couplings

V.2.1
Definitions

In general we can expand the operator
Q_{n\mathbf{k}}
, which couples to
a single phonon, in powers
of the magnon operators,

\displaystyle Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{\ell,q_{1},z}\mathcal{A}^{n,\ell|q_{1}q}_{\mathbf{k}}\,e^{ik_{z}z}b^{q_{1}}_{\ell,{\mathbf{k}},z}

(57)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p},\ell,\ell^{\prime}\\
q_{1},q_{2},z\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\mathcal{B}^{n,\ell_{1},\ell_{2}|q_{1}q_{2}q}_{\mathbf{k};\mathbf{p}}~e^{ik_{z}z}b^{q_{1}}_{\ell_{1},\mathbf{p}+\frac{q}{2}\mathbf{k},z}b^{q_{2}}_{\ell_{2},-\mathbf{p}+\frac{q}{2}\mathbf{k},z}.

Note that while the phonons are three-dimensional excitations, and
hence have a three-dimensional momentum
\mathbf{k}
, the spin operators
(and hence magnons) only have two dimensional momenta. We will make
use of the following:
\mathbf{k}=\underline{\mathbf{k}}+k_{z}\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{z}
,
where
\underline{\mathbf{k}}
is the projection of
\mathbf{k}
onto the
k_{z}=0

plane and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{z}
is the unit vector along
z
. A phonon is
coupled to the sum of spin operators in all layers—we have here
introduced the explicit label
z
for the layer. Because the
spins in different layers are completely uncorrelated, there are
however no cross-terms involving
b
operators from different layers,
and in correlation functions the sums over
z
will collapse to
independent correlators within each layer, which are all identical to
one another. When possible, we will therefore take
z=0
and suppress
this index.

The naïve leading term in Eq. (
57
) is the single
magnon one
\mathcal{A}
, linear in
b_{\ell,\mathbf{k}}^{\vphantom{\dagger}}
and
b_{\ell,\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}
operators (notations defined below). This results in a quadratic mixing term in
the Hamiltonian, hybridizing phonons and magnons. Being quadratic, it
is trivially diagonalized, and has been considered by several
authors. Generally, such coupling has little effect except when it
is resonant, i.e. near a crossing point of the decoupled magnon and
phonon bands. Since such a crossing is highly constrained by momentum
and energy matching, it occurs in a narrow region of phase
space, if at all, and is likely to be unimportant for transport. It
in any case does not give rise to scattering, the focus of this work.
We therefore henceforth neglect the
\mathcal{A}
contribution.

Non-trivial scattering processes arise from the second order term in
the magnon field expansion of
Q_{n\mathbf{k}}
, parametrized by
\mathcal{B}
.
Here as elsewhere we introduce particle-hole indices
q_{1},q_{2}\in{+,-}
, such that in particular

\displaystyle b_{\ell,\mathbf{p},z}^{+}=b^{\dagger}_{\ell,\mathbf{p},z},

\displaystyle b_{\ell,\mathbf{p},z}^{-}=b^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{\ell,-\mathbf{p},z}.

(58)

Notice the minus sign in the momentum in the second relation. This
means generally that

\left(b^{q}_{\ell,\mathbf{p},z}\right)^{\dagger}=b^{-q}_{\ell,-\mathbf{p},z}.

(59)

To make the
coefficients unambiguous, we choose the symmetrized form

\mathcal{B}^{n,\ell_{1},\ell_{2}|q_{1}q_{2}q}_{\mathbf{k};\mathbf{p}}=\mathcal{B}^{n,\ell_{2},\ell_{1}|q_{2}q_{1}q}_{\mathbf{k};-\mathbf{p}}.

(60)

Demanding that
Q^{+}_{n\mathbf{k}}=(Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{-})^{\dagger}
implies that

\mathcal{B}^{n,\ell_{1},\ell_{2}|q_{1}q_{2}+}_{\mathbf{k};\mathbf{p}}=\left(\mathcal{B}^{n,\ell_{2},\ell_{1}|-q_{2}-q_{1}-}_{\mathbf{k};\mathbf{p}}\right)^{*}.

(61)

If the phonon mode
n
which
Q^{q}_{n{\mathbf{k}}}
is coupled to is

C_{2}
invariant, then only terms with
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
are non-zero.
In Sec.
V.3.1
, we will introduce a concrete and general model of spin-lattice couplings, and see that within this model, almost all interactions obey this selection rule. In particular, off-diagonal terms with
\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
arise only from the
\Lambda_{6,7}^{(\xi)}
couplings defined in Eq. (
V.3.1
), which are furthermore smaller in magnitude than other couplings as they are related to magnetic anisotropy.

V.2.2
Diagonal scattering rate

Contributions to the first-order longitudinal scattering rate, Eq. (
34
),
can be computed exactly using Wick’s theorem. To do so we use the
free particle two point function, which in the notation of
Eq. (
58
) is

\displaystyle\left\langle b^{q_{1}}_{\ell_{1},\mathbf{p}_{1},z_{1}}(t_{1})b^{q_{2}}_{\ell_{2},\mathbf{p}_{2},z_{2}}(t_{2})\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\delta_{z_{1},z_{2}}\delta_{q_{1},-q_{2}}\delta_{\mathbf{p}_{1}+\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{0}}
