Note that, in a time-reversal symmetric system, these satisfy the symmetry

\mathcal{W}^{[1,1];[1,1],ss^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=ss^{\prime}\mathcal{W}^{[1,1];[1,1],s^{\prime}s}_{n-\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}},

(195)

reflecting the role of
\pm i\eta
in the denominators in terms of causality.

C.4.3
Detailed balance

Using the method of the previous subsection Sec.
C.2.3
, we
show the following (“anti-”)detailed-balance relations

W^{[1,1];[1,1],(a),ss^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=ss^{\prime}~W^{[1,1];[1,1],(a),s^{\prime}s}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-q^{\prime},n\mathbf{k}-q}e^{-\beta(q\omega_{\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})},

(196)

W^{[1,1];[1,1],(b),ss^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=ss^{\prime}~W^{[1,1];[1,1],(b),s^{\prime}s}_{n\mathbf{k}-q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-q^{\prime}}e^{-\beta(q\omega_{\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}.

(197)

From this, the same holds for the symmetrized in
n\mathbf{k}q\leftrightarrow n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}
scattering rate:

\mathcal{W}^{[1,1];[1,1],ss^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=ss^{\prime}~e^{-\beta(q\omega_{\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}\mathcal{W}^{[1,1];[1,1],ss^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}-q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-q^{\prime}}.

(198)

We now identify

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{\rm uc}\sum_{s=\pm}\mathcal{W}^{[1,1];[1,1],s,-s}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}},

(199)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{\rm uc}\sum_{s=\pm}\mathcal{W}^{[1,1];[1,1],ss}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}},

(200)

complete expressions of which are given in the main text.

By construction, these enforce

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,[1,1];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\sigma~e^{-\beta(q\omega_{\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,[1,1];[1,1],-q-q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

(201)

where
\sigma=\oplus
(resp.
\sigma=\ominus
) indicates
that
\mathfrak{W}
enforces detailed balance (resp. “anti-detailed balance”).

C.4.4
All contributions

As mentioned at the beginning of this subsection, other terms of order

{\lambda}^{4}
contribute to the thermal conductivity at
second Born’s order. In the following, we use the shorthand

\llbracket A(-t),B(t^{\prime})\rrbracket={\rm sign}(t+t^{\prime})[A(-t),B(t^{\prime})]

and
\fint_{t,\{t_{j}\}}
(resp.
\fint^{\prime}_{t,\{t_{j}\}}
),
j=1,..,l
, denotes the set of
1+l

inverse Fourier transforms evaluated once at

\Sigma_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and
l
times at

\Delta_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
, i.e.
\fint_{t,\{t_{j}\}}=\int\text{d}t\text{d}t_{1}..\text{d}t_{l}e^{i\Sigma_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}t}e^{i\Delta_{n\mathbf{k}q}^{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}(t_{1}+..+t_{l})}
,
(resp. once at
q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}
and
l
times at

q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
,
i.e.
\fint_{t,\{t_{j}\}}^{\prime}=\int\text{d}t\text{d}t_{1}..\text{d}t_{l}e^{iq\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}t}e^{iq^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(t_{1}+..+t_{l})}
).

One contribution comes from the “cross-term”

2\mathfrak{Re}\{(T^{[2]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}})^{*}T^{[1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\}
, which contributes
to the scattering rates in the form of

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2N_{\rm uc}^{1/2}}{\hbar^{4}}~\mathfrak{Im}\fint_{t,t_{1}}\left\langle Q^{-q,-q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t)\{Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}(-t_{1}),Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}(t_{1})\}\right\rangle

(202)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\oplus,[1,1];[2],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2N_{\rm uc}^{1/2}}{\hbar^{4}}~\mathfrak{Im}\fint_{t,t_{1}}\left\langle Q^{-q,-q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t)\llbracket Q_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{q^{\prime}}(-t_{1}),Q_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}(t_{1})\rrbracket\right\rangle,

(203)

The last contribution comes from considering the second
Born’s order matrix element

\displaystyle T^{[2,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}=\pm}\left(N^{i}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1+q^{\prime}}{2}\right)\sqrt{N^{i}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\tfrac{1+q}{2}}~\mathds{I}({\mathtt{i}_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathtt{f}_{p}})

(204)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathtt{m}_{s}}\left\{\frac{\langle\mathtt{f}_{s}|Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\mathtt{m}_{s}\rangle\langle\mathtt{m}_{s}|Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\mathtt{i}_{s}\rangle}{E_{\mathtt{f}_{s}}-E_{\mathtt{m}_{s}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+i\eta}+\frac{\langle\mathtt{f}_{s}|Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\mathtt{m}_{s}\rangle\langle\mathtt{m}_{s}|Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|\mathtt{i}_{s}\rangle}{E_{\mathtt{i}_{s}}-E_{\mathtt{m}_{s}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+i\eta}\right\},

which contains two-phonon operators, of order
{\lambda}^{2}
.
At order
{\lambda}^{4}
, it is thus involved in the “cross-term”

2~\mathfrak{Re}\{(T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}})^{*}T^{[2,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\}
,
which contributes to scattering rates in the form of

\mathfrak{W}^{[2,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\sum_{s=\pm}\mathfrak{W}^{[2,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|s}
, where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{[2,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N_{\rm uc}^{1/2}}{\hbar^{4}}\mathfrak{Im}\fint_{t,t_{1}}^{\prime}\left\langle Q^{-q}_{n\mathbf{k}}(-t)[{\rm sign}(t_{1})]^{\frac{1-s}{2}}\llbracket Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t_{1}),Q^{qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(0)\rrbracket_{s}\right\rangle,

(205)

and we recall the shorthand
\llbracket A,B\rrbracket_{\pm}=AB\pm BA
.

The first two terms enforce the usual, “two-phonon”, (anti-)detailed balance relations

\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,[2];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=\sigma~e^{-\beta(q\omega_{\mathbf{k}}+q^{\prime}\omega_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})}\mathfrak{W}^{\sigma,[2];[1,1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}},

(206)

with
\sigma=\oplus,\ominus
.
Meanwhile, the last term satisfies “one-phonon” (anti-)detailed balance,

\mathfrak{W}^{[2,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|s}=s\,e^{-q\beta\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}\mathfrak{W}^{[2,1];[1],-q-q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|s},

(207)

where we used a different notation (
s=\pm
as a lower index) to emphasize
the difference with the other terms derived hereabove.

C.5
Computation at third Born’s order

The only third-order element of
T_{\mathtt{i}\mapsto\mathtt{f}}
which can appear in a
term of order
\lambda^{4}
in
|T_{\mathtt{i}\mapsto\mathtt{f}}|^{2}
is

\displaystyle T^{[1,1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\mathbf{k}q,n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}q^{\prime}}\sqrt{N^{i}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\tfrac{1+q}{2}}\left(N^{i}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1+q^{\prime}}{2}\right)~\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{q\cdot n\mathbf{k}}{\longrightarrow}{f_{p}})\times\sum_{m_{s},m^{\prime}_{s}}\left\{\tfrac{\langle f_{s}|Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|m^{\prime}_{s}\rangle\langle m^{\prime}_{s}|Q^{q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|m_{s}\rangle\langle m_{s}|Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}|i_{s}\rangle}{(E_{i_{s}}-E_{m_{s}}-q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+i\eta)(E_{f_{s}}-E_{m^{\prime}_{s}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}}+i\eta)}\right.

(208)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{\langle f_{s}|Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|m^{\prime}_{s}\rangle\langle m^{\prime}_{s}|Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}|m_{s}\rangle\langle m_{s}|Q^{q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|i_{s}\rangle}{(E_{i_{s}}-E_{m_{s}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}}+i\eta)(E_{f_{s}}-E_{m^{\prime}_{s}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}}+i\eta)}\left.+\tfrac{\langle f_{s}|Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}|m^{\prime}_{s}\rangle\langle m^{\prime}_{s}|Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|m_{s}\rangle\langle m_{s}|Q^{q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|i_{s}\rangle}{(E_{i_{s}}-E_{m_{s}}-q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}}+i\eta)(E_{f_{s}}-E_{m^{\prime}_{s}}+q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}+i\eta)}\right\},

which is involved in the scattering rate

\mathfrak{W}^{[1,1,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=2\mathfrak{Re}\left[(T^{[1,1,1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}})^{*}T^{[1]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\right]=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}=\pm}\mathfrak{W}^{[1,1,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|ss^{\prime}},

(209)

where we denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{[1,1,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|ss^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{2\hbar^{4}}\mathfrak{Re}\fint_{t,t_{1},t_{2}}^{\prime}\left\langle Q^{-q}_{n\mathbf{k}}(-t)\left\lgroup Q^{-q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(-t_{1}),\,Q^{q}_{n\mathbf{k}}(0),\,Q^{q^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}(t_{2})\right\rgroup_{s,s^{\prime}}\right\rangle,

(210)

\displaystyle\left\lgroup A(t),B(t^{\prime}),C(t^{\prime\prime})\right\rgroup_{s,s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\text{sign}(t-t^{\prime})]^{\tfrac{1-s}{2}}[\text{sign}(t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime})]^{\tfrac{1-s^{\prime}}{2}}\left(\vphantom{\int}A(t)B(t^{\prime})C(t^{\prime\prime})+sB(t^{\prime})A(t)C(t^{\prime\prime})+s^{\prime}A(t)C(t^{\prime\prime})B(t^{\prime})\right).

This term enforces an unusual version of (anti-)detailed balance,
namely

\mathfrak{W}^{[1,1,1];[1],qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|ss^{\prime}}=ss^{\prime}e^{-q\beta\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}\mathfrak{W}^{[1,1,1];[1],-q,q^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|ss^{\prime}},

(211)

where the index
q^{\prime}
remains inchanged.

Appendix D
Generalizations

D.1
Generalized model and higher perturbative orders

To describe
the interaction between phonon and another degree of freedom, we
introduce general coupling terms between phonon annihilation
(creation)
operators
a^{(\dagger)}_{n\mathbf{k}}
and general, for now
unspecified, fields
Q^{\{q_{j}\}}_{\{n_{j},\mathbf{k}_{j}\}}
which
are operators acting in their own Hilbert
space, i.e. we write
H^{\prime}=\sum_{l}H^{\prime}_{[l]}
, with

H^{\prime}_{[l]}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}^{l}}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}\sum_{\{q_{i}=\pm\}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{m}a_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}^{q_{j}}\Big)Q^{\{q_{j}\}}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}}.

(212)

In this expression,
m
is the number of phonon creation-annihilation operators
coupled to
Q^{\{q_{j}\}_{i=1..l}}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{i=1..l}}
. In terms of the perturbative expansion introduced
in the main text and the other appendices, this means

Q^{\{q_{j}\}_{i=1..l}}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{i=1..l}}\sim\lambda^{l}
;
note that since the perturbative expansion is considered (formally) up to
infinite
order in this appendix,
we make this specification only for the sake of clarity.
To avoid ambiguities, we assume that all the
n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}
indices involved in a given term of
H^{\prime}_{[l]}

are distinct; this is correct in the thermodynamic limit.
Note also that, for the sake of clarity in the following developments,
the normalization factors of
N_{\rm uc}
are not
defined following the same convention as in the rest of the paper.

In what follows, we take special notations for the first two indices:

n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1}\equiv n\mathbf{k}
,
n_{2}\mathbf{k}_{2}\equiv n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
.
Using the model Eq. (
68
) and following the general procedure
described in Sec.
C
and in the main text, one can then
(at least formally) derive the collision integral which always takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}^{p}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=2,..,p}}\sum_{\{q_{i}\}_{i=1,..,p}}q_{1}

(213)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left[\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\overline{N}_{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}}+\tfrac{q_{i}+1}{2}\right)\right]W^{(p),\{q_{i}\}}_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}},

where the index
(p)
denotes a term of order
\lambda^{2p}
.
The scattering rate
W^{(p),\{q_{i}\}}_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}=W^{(p),\{q_{i}\}_{i=1,..,p}}_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=1,..,p}}

is the sum of all the scattering rates of the
[l_{1},..,l_{m}];[l^{\prime}_{1},..,l^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}]
kind (according to the nomenclature introduced
in the main text) such that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}l_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}l^{\prime}_{j}=2p
.
In terms of physical process, each of these terms corresponds to the interference between two
scattering channels,
[l_{1},..,l_{m}]
and
[l^{\prime}_{1},..,l^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}]
, such that in all,
2p
phonon creations or annihilations occur
between the initial
\mathtt{i}
and final
\mathtt{f}
states.
Note that in the present paper, we compute explicitly this expansion up to
p=2
.

We then expand the phonon average populations as

\overline{N}_{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}}=N^{\rm eq}_{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}}+\delta\overline{N}_{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}}
.
Following Eq.(
11
), the diagonal scattering rate is obtained as

D_{n\mathbf{k}}=-\partial_{\overline{N}_{n\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}\Big|_{\overline{N}=N^{\rm eq}}
. It can be decomposed
as
D_{n\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(p)}
, where

\displaystyle D_{n\mathbf{k}}^{(p)}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{\rm uc}^{p}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=2,..,p}}\sum_{\{q_{i}\}_{i=1,..,p}}q_{1}

(214)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left[\prod_{i=2}^{p}\left(N^{\rm eq}_{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}}+\tfrac{q_{i}+1}{2}\right)\right]W^{(p),\{q_{i}\}}_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}.

Similarly, the off-diagonal scattering rate is obtained as

M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\partial_{\overline{N}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}\Big|_{\overline{N}=N^{\rm eq}}
. It can be decomposed
as
M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\sum_{p=2}^{\infty}M_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{(p)}
, where

\displaystyle M^{(p)}_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}^{p}}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=3,..,p}}\sum_{\{q_{i}\}_{i=1,..,p}}q_{1}

(215)

\displaystyle\times\left(N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}}+\tfrac{q_{1}+1}{2}\right)\left[\prod_{i=3}^{p}\left(N^{\rm eq}_{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}}+\tfrac{q_{i}+1}{2}\right)\right]W^{(p),\{q_{i}\}}_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}}.

Like in the equations for
p=1,2
derived explicitly in Appendix
C
,

q_{1}
always factorizes in the collision integral, as the change in number of
n\mathbf{k}
phonons
due to the scattering event.

D.2
Special properties of first Born’s order

D.2.1
Definitions and basic results

At first order of the Born expansion, all contributions to the collision integral are “semiclassical”,
in the sense defined in Sec.
III.4.3
;
i.e. an operator
Q^{q_{1},..,q_{l}}_{n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1},..,n_{l}\mathbf{k}_{l}}
does only appear
in the collision integral as
|Q^{q_{1},..,q_{l}}_{n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1},..,n_{l}\mathbf{k}_{l}}|^{2}
.

To make this statement more precise, we rewrite

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{[l]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{\rm uc}^{l}}}\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=1..l}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{r}a_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}^{+}\Big)\Big(\prod_{j=r+1}^{l}a_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}^{-}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{r!(l-r)!}}\,Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1}..n_{r}\mathbf{k}_{r}|n_{r+1}\mathbf{k}_{r+1}..n_{l}\mathbf{k}_{l}},

(216)

where the upper indices of
Q
are
r
times ’
+
’ and
l-r
times ’
-
’,
and
Q
is by definition symmetric under permutation of its lower indices in the two blocks

\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=1,..,r}
and
\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=r+1,..,l}
separately.
Hermiticity is guaranteed by
Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1}..n_{r}\mathbf{k}_{r}|n_{r+1}\mathbf{k}_{r+1}..n_{l}\mathbf{k}_{l}}=(Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{n_{r+1}\mathbf{k}_{r+1}..n_{l}\mathbf{k}_{l}|n_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1}..n_{r}\mathbf{k}_{r}})^{\dagger}
.
Note that at first Born’s order, distinct scattering channels
[l]
and
[l^{\prime}]
do not interfere for
l\neq l^{\prime}
;
one can thus study independently the contribution of each
H^{\prime}_{[l]}
to the collision integral.

The contribution to the squared T-matrix obtained from
H^{\prime}_{[l]}
at first Born’s order is

\displaystyle\Big|T^{[l]}_{\mathtt{i}\rightarrow\mathtt{f}}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}^{l}}\sum_{r=0}^{l}\frac{1}{r!(l-r)!}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=1..l}}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{r}(N^{\mathtt{i}}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}+1)\right]\left[\prod_{j=r+1}^{l}(N^{\mathtt{i}}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}})\right]

(217)

\displaystyle\times\mathds{I}({i_{p}}\overset{+\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}}{\underset{-\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}{\xrightarrow{\hskip 42.67912pt}}}{f_{p}})\,\langle i_{s}|Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}}|f_{s}\rangle\langle f_{s}|Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}|i_{s}\rangle,

Summing over all scattering channels,
the first Born’s order contribution to the collision integral is

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}^{l}}\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=1,..,l}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\overline{N}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}+1)\prod_{j=r+1}^{l}(\overline{N}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}})\left(\frac{r\delta_{n,n_{1}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}_{1}}}{r!(l-r)!}-\frac{(l-r)\delta_{n,n_{r+1}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}_{r+1}}}{r!(l-r)!}\right)W^{[l];[l]}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}},

(218)

where

\displaystyle W^{[l];[l]}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}te^{-i\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}-\sum_{j=r+1}^{l}\omega_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}\right)t}\left\langle Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}}(t)\,Q^{+,..,+|-,..,-}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}\right\rangle.

(219)

Following the same steps as in Sec.
C.2.3
,
it is easy to see that
W^{[l];[l]}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}

always
enforces detailed-balance, namely

\displaystyle W^{[l];[l]}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\beta\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\omega_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}-\sum_{j=r+1}^{l}\omega_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times W^{[l];[l]}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}}.

(220)

We now prove two important properties of the collision integral,
as obtained from first Born’s order, which derive therefrom.

D.2.2
No equilibrium current

The equilibrium current is due to

O_{n\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{C}_{n\mathbf{k}}[\overline{N}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mapsto N^{\rm eq}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}]
, the constant
term in the collision integral.

In the present case, by performing a change of index
r\mapsto l-r
in the second term of

(\dots-\dots)
in Eq. (
218
), and resorting to the detailed balance relation Eq. (
D.2.1
)
and taking
N^{\rm eq}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{e^{\beta\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}-1}
, one can easily
show that

O^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k}}=\mathcal{C}^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k}}[\overline{N}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}\mapsto N^{\rm eq}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}]=0.

(221)

This means that no shift of the phonons’ energies
is needed at first Born’s order to guarantee cancellation of the equilibrium current.

D.2.3
No phonon Hall effect

The off-diagonal contribution to the collision matrix at first Born’s order,

M^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}=\partial_{\overline{N}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\mathcal{C}^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k}}
, reads

\displaystyle M^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N_{\rm uc}^{l}}\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{\{n_{i}\mathbf{k}_{i}\}_{i=1,..,l}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(N^{\rm eq}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}}+1)\prod_{j=r+1}^{l}(N^{\rm eq}_{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}})\,W^{[l];[l]}_{\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{1}^{r}|\{n_{j}\mathbf{k}_{j}\}_{r+1}^{l}}

(222)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{1}{r!(l-r)!}\left\lgroup r\delta_{n,n_{1}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}_{1}}\Big((r-1)\delta_{n^{\prime},n_{2}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}_{2}}+(l-r)\delta_{n^{\prime},n_{r+1}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}_{r+1}}\Big)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-(l-r)\delta_{n,n_{r+1}}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}_{r+1}}\Big(r\delta_{n^{\prime},n_{1}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}_{1}}+(l-r-1)\delta_{n^{\prime},n_{r+2}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}_{r+2}}\Big)\right\rgroup

After some algebra, following essentially the same steps as outlined hereabove,
it is possible to show that

M^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}e^{\beta\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}(N^{\rm eq}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})^{2}=M^{\mathtt{1B}}_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n\mathbf{k}}e^{\beta\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}(N^{\rm eq}_{n\mathbf{k}})^{2}.

(223)

This, as was illustrated several times in the main text and the appendices,
entails that
M^{\mathtt{1B}}
does not contribute to
\kappa_{\rm H}
– see Eq.
152
.
We have thus shown that no contribution to the thermal Hall conductivity can possibly come
from first Born’s order, regardless of the number of phonon operators in the Hamiltonian and of the
nature of the operators
Q
to which they are coupled.

Appendix E
Application—further technical details

E.1
Solving the delta functions

In order to solve the two simulaneous delta functions, we use the
following rewriting of
\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{64\pi^{2}}{\hbar^{4}N_{\rm uc}^{2d}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}}\sum_{\{\ell_{i},q_{i}\}}\mathcal{F}^{\ell_{3},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}|q_{4},q_{1},q_{2}}_{\mathbf{p},q\mathbf{k},q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\mathfrak{Im}\Bigg\{{\mathcal{B}}^{n\ell_{2}\ell_{3}|q_{2}q_{3}q}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{p}+\frac{1}{2}q\mathbf{k}+q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{B}}^{n^{\prime}\ell_{3}\ell_{1}|-q_{3}q_{1}q^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}+\frac{1}{2}q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

(224)

\displaystyle{\rm PP}\Bigg[\frac{{\mathcal{B}}^{n\ell_{1}\ell_{4}|-q_{1}q_{4}-q}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{p}+\frac{1}{2}q\mathbf{k}}{\mathcal{B}}^{n^{\prime}\ell_{4}\ell_{2}|-q_{4}-q_{2}-q^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}+q\mathbf{k}+\frac{1}{2}q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{2v_{\rm m}q_{1}\left(\frac{q_{1}q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}{v_{\rm m}}+\hat{\Omega}_{\ell_{1},\mathbf{p}}-q_{1}q_{4}\hat{\Omega}_{\ell_{4},\mathbf{p}+q\mathbf{k}}\right)}+(n,q,\mathbf{k},q_{4})\leftrightarrow(n^{\prime},q^{\prime},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-q_{4})\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\delta\left(v_{\rm m}q_{1}\left[\frac{q_{1}\Sigma_{n\mathbf{k}n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}^{qq^{\prime}}}{v_{\rm m}}+\hat{\Omega}_{\ell_{1},\mathbf{p}}+q_{1}q_{2}\hat{\Omega}_{\ell_{2},\mathbf{p}+q\mathbf{k}+q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\right]\right)\delta\left(-2v_{\rm m}q_{1}\left[\frac{q_{1}q^{\prime}\omega_{n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{v_{\rm m}}+\hat{\Omega}_{\ell_{1},\mathbf{p}}-q_{1}q_{3}\hat{\Omega}_{\ell_{3},\mathbf{p}+q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\right]\right),

where
\hat{\Omega}_{\ell,\mathbf{p}}=\Omega_{\ell,\mathbf{p}}/v_{\rm m}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\ell_{3},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}|q_{4},q_{1},q_{2}}_{\mathbf{p},q\mathbf{k},q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q_{4}\left(2n_{\rm B}(\Omega_{\ell_{3},\mathbf{p}+q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})+1\right)\left(2n_{\rm B}(\Omega_{\ell_{1},\mathbf{p}})+q_{1}+1\right)\left(2n_{\rm B}(\Omega_{\ell_{2},\mathbf{p}+q\mathbf{k}+q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})+q_{2}+1\right)

(225)

is a product of thermal factors (note

\hat{\mathcal{F}}=q_{1}\mathcal{F}
, cf. Eq. (
94
)). Now collapsing the delta functions, we
can write:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}^{\ominus,qq^{\prime}}_{n\mathbf{k},n^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8\mathfrak{a}^{2}}{\hbar^{4}v_{\rm m}^{2}}\sum_{j}\sum_{\ell,\{q_{i}\}}\mathfrak{Im}\Bigg\{{\mathcal{B}}^{n\ell\ell|q_{2}q_{3}q}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{p}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}q\mathbf{k}+q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{B}}^{n^{\prime}\ell\ell|-q_{3}q_{1}q^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}{\rm PP}\Bigg[\frac{{\mathcal{B}}^{n\ell\ell|-q_{1}q_{4}-q}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{p}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}q\mathbf{k}}{\mathcal{B}}^{n^{\prime}\ell\ell|-q_{4}-q_{2}-q^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}_{j}+q\mathbf{k}+\frac{1}{2}q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{2v_{\rm m}q_{1}\left(\frac{q_{1}q\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}{v_{\rm m}}+\hat{\Omega}_{\ell,\mathbf{p}_{j}}-q_{1}q_{4}\hat{\Omega}_{\ell,\mathbf{p}_{j}+q\mathbf{k}}\right)}

(226)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(n,q,\mathbf{k},q_{4})\leftrightarrow(n^{\prime},q^{\prime},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},-q_{4})\Bigg]\Bigg\}~J_{\mathfrak{W}}(\mathbf{p}_{j})\mathcal{F}^{\ell,\ell,\ell|q_{4},q_{1},q_{2}}_{\mathbf{p}_{j},q\mathbf{k},q^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}},

where, when they exist, the solutions,
j=0,..,3
take the form
(recall
\mathbf{v}_{i}=\underline{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
,

\mathbf{w}_{i}=\underline{\mathbf{w}}_{i}
,
\mathbf{p}_{j}=\underline{\mathbf{p}}_{j}
)

\mathbf{p}_{j}=t_{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}\mathbf{v}_{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}+u_{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}^{(\widetilde{j\;[2]})}\mathbf{w}_{\lfloor j/2\rfloor},

(227)

where, for
i=0,1

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{2}\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+(-1)^{i}a_{1}\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{2},\hskip 170.71652pt\mathbf{w}_{i}=\mathbf{\hat{z}}\times\mathbf{v}_{i},

(228)

\displaystyle t_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{1}^{2}+(-1)^{i}a_{1}\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{2}^{2}-a_{1}a_{2}(a_{1}+(-1)^{i}a_{2})}{2\mathbf{v}_{i}^{2}},\hskip 71.13188ptu_{i}^{(\pm)}=\frac{-B_{i}\pm\sqrt{B_{i}^{2}-4A_{i}C_{i}}}{2A_{i}},

(229)

\displaystyle A_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4a_{i+1}^{2}(\mathbf{v}_{i}^{2}-(\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{1}\wedge\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{2})^{2}),

(230)

\displaystyle B_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{i}4a_{i+1}(\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{1}\wedge\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{2})(a_{i+1}^{2}-\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1}^{2}+2(\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1})t_{i}),

\displaystyle C_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(a_{i+1}(a_{i+1}-2\delta_{\ell})-\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1}^{2}\right)\left(a_{i+1}(a_{i+1}+2\delta_{\ell})-\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1}^{2}\right)-4(a_{i+1}^{2}-\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1}^{2})(\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1})t_{i}+4(a_{i+1}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{2}-(\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\underline{\mathbf{k}}_{i+1})^{2})t_{i}^{2},

and
J_{\mathfrak{W}}(\mathbf{p}_{j})
is given in the main text, Eq. (
96
).

E.2
Choice of polarization vectors

Below, we enumerate possible explicit choices for a basis of polarization vectors

(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,\mathbf{k}},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1,\mathbf{k}},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2,\mathbf{k}})
.
In the numerical calculations, we use choice 2.

E.2.1
Choice 1

A simple choice is that of momentum-independent polarization vectors,
which can be, for example:

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}(\mathbf{k})=\mathbf{\hat{x}}
,

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}(\mathbf{k})=\mathbf{\hat{y}}
,

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}(\mathbf{k})=\mathbf{\hat{z}}
.

E.2.2
Choice 2

Below, we describe the choice of polarization vectors used in the numerical implementation.
Its polarization vectors
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n,\mathbf{k}}
form an orthonormal basis in which

\mathbf{k}
points along the
[1,1,1]
axis, so that
\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n,\mathbf{k}}=\frac{|\mathbf{k}|}{\sqrt{3}}~\forall n
.
This, as explained in the main text, ensures that the structure factor

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}^{\alpha,\beta}
does not vanish for
\alpha=\beta
,
corresponding to the largest coupling constants
\Lambda_{1..5}
(as opposed to anisotropic
\Lambda_{6,7}
for which
\alpha\neq\beta
).

The starting point is the orthonormal basis made of three vectors
\mathbf{e}_{n},n=0,1,2
, defined as

\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\left(\sqrt{2}\mathbf{\hat{x}}+\mathbf{\hat{z}}\right)\\
\mathbf{e}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\left(-\mathbf{\hat{x}}+\sqrt{3}\mathbf{\hat{y}}+\sqrt{2}\mathbf{\hat{z}}\right)\\
\mathbf{e}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\left(-\mathbf{\hat{x}}-\sqrt{3}\mathbf{\hat{y}}+\sqrt{2}\mathbf{\hat{z}}\right)\end{cases};

(231)

in this basis,
\mathbf{\hat{z}}=[1,1,1]
.
To rotate the
\mathbf{\hat{z}}
axis into
\mathbf{\hat{k}}
’s direction, we
define the polar angles of
\mathbf{\hat{k}}
,

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\arccos(k_{z}/|\mathbf{k}|),

(232)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Arg}(k_{x}+\text{i}k_{y}),

(233)

so that a good choice for the three polarization vectors is

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n,\mathbf{k}}=\text{i}\,R_{\mathbf{\hat{z}}}(\phi)\cdot R_{\mathbf{\hat{y}}}(\theta)\cdot{\rm diag}\left[\mathsf{s}(\mathbf{k}),1,1\right]\cdot\mathbf{e}_{n}

(234)

for
n=0,1,2
.
In the above, we defined
R_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mu}}}(\gamma)
to be
the direct rotation matrix around the
\mu
axis by an angle
\gamma
,
and we used the “sign” function

\mathsf{s}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if}\quad\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{+}\\
-1&\text{if}\quad\mathbf{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{-}\end{cases}

(235)

with respect to two domains
\mathcal{D}_{\pm}
, corresponding (up to unimportant details
in a set of null measure contained in the
k_{z}=0
plane) to the
“upper” (
k_{z}>0
) and “lower” (
k_{z}<0
) halves of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and more precisely
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathbf{k}\;\big|\;k_{z}>0\text{ or }\big(k_{z}=0

(236)

\displaystyle\text{ and }(k_{y}>0\text{ or }(k_{y}=0\text{ and }k_{x}>0))\big)\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathbf{k}\;\big|\;k_{z}<0\text{ or }\big(k_{z}=0

(237)

\displaystyle\text{ and }(k_{y}<0\text{ or }(k_{y}=0\text{ and }k_{x}<0))\big)\Big\}

such that
\mathbb{R}^{3}=\mathcal{D}_{+}\cup\mathcal{D}_{-}\cup\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.
The role of this
\mathsf{s}(\mathbf{k})
function is to help ensure that this choice
of polarizations enforces
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n}(-\mathbf{k})=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n}(\mathbf{k})^{*}
, as
well as all the tetragonal symmetry group of the crystal.
This last statement means that under a symmetry operation
g
belonging
to
D_{4h}
the symmetry group of the crystal, they transform as

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n}(g\cdot\mathbf{k})=\sum_{n^{\prime}}c^{g}_{nn^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})\,g\cdot\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}),

(238)

where
g\cdot
denotes the action of
g
on a vector,
and most importantly the
c^{g}_{nn^{\prime}}
coefficient either is
\delta_{nn^{\prime}}
or
exchanges the
n=1
and
n=2
polarizations, depending on whether
\mathbf{k}
is in a high-symmetry position. Indeed
n=1,2
are constructed degenerate
(as eigenvectors of the dynamical matrix) at the high-symmetry
planes and axes of the Brillouin zone. See Ref.
Maradudin and Vosko 1968
for details and further discussion on the
behavior of polarization vectors under symmetry operations.

E.2.3
Choice 3

One may also
use the Hall-plane-dependent basis for the polarization vectors and
label
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{n\mathbf{k}}
, assuming
\mu\nu
is the Hall
plane,
\rho
is the direction transverse to the plane, and

\mu\nu\rho
forms a direct orthonormal basis:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\mathbf{k}/|\mathbf{k}|\quad\mbox{(longitudinal)},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1,\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\frac{\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{\rho}\times\mathbf{k}}{|\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{\rho}\times\mathbf{k}|}\quad\mbox{(transverse, in Hall plane)},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2,\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0,\mathbf{k}}\times\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1,\mathbf{k}}=\frac{k^{\rho}\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{2}\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{\rho}}{|\mathbf{k}||\hat{\mathbf{u}}_{\rho}\times\mathbf{k}|}\quad\mbox{(transverse)}.

Then,
if

\alpha,\beta,\mu,\nu,\rho\in\{x,y,z\}
, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{q;\alpha\beta}_{0\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{0\mathbf{k}}}}\frac{2qi}{|\mathbf{k}|}k^{\alpha}k^{\beta},

(240)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{q;\alpha\beta}_{1\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{1\mathbf{k}}}}\frac{qi}{|\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{\rho}\times\mathbf{k}|}\left(k^{\mu}(k^{\alpha}\delta_{\beta,\nu}+k^{\beta}\delta_{\alpha,\nu})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-k^{\nu}(k^{\alpha}\delta_{\beta,\mu}+k^{\beta}\delta_{\alpha,\mu})\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{q;\alpha\beta}_{2\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{2\mathbf{k}}}}\frac{-1}{|\mathbf{k}||\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{\rho}\times\mathbf{k}|}\left(\mathbf{k}^{2}(k^{\alpha}\delta_{\beta,\rho}+k^{\beta}\delta_{\alpha,\rho})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-2k^{\alpha}k^{\beta}k^{\rho}\right),

so that

\mathcal{L}_{0}\propto\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{k}
,

\mathcal{L}_{1}\propto[\mathbf{k}\times(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{k})]^{\rho}
,

\mathcal{L}_{2}\propto[\mathbf{k}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{k})-(\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{k})\mathbf{k}]^{\rho}
.

For this choice of polarization vectors, the phonon-magnon coupling constants can be decomposed in such a way that
their behavior under operations of the
D_{4h}
point-group defined in the
(\mu,\nu,\rho)
basis becomes transparent,
in other words in terms of the basis harmonics of the “Hall geometry” point-group.
Note that, because the magnetic space group of the system is a priori independent of the symmetries associated
with the choice of “Hall geometry,” the coefficients of the harmonics need not be
independent. (
In the the square lattice case discussed here
, some of
the symmetries of the system coincide with those of the Hall geometry, so that these coefficients are not entirely
independent. Note that this causes additional constraints for the existence of a nonzero Hall effect.)

E.3
Numerical implementation

We define
\hat{\lambda}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2};\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{\alpha\beta}_{\tilde{\xi}\ell_{1}+\bar{\xi}+1,\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}\ell_{2}+\bar{\xi}^{\prime}+1;\xi\xi^{\prime}}
,
so that, in particular,

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{1,1}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2};\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{\alpha\beta}_{\ell_{1}+1,\ell_{2}+1;11},

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{0,0}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2};\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{\alpha\beta}_{\bar{\ell}_{1}+1,\bar{\ell}_{2}+1;00},

\displaystyle\hat{\lambda}_{1,0}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2};\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{\alpha\beta}_{\ell_{1}+1,\bar{\ell}_{2}+1;10},

(241)

(note the bars) and

\mathcal{L}_{n\mathbf{k};\xi,\xi^{\prime}}^{q,\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}={\rm Tr}\left[({\boldsymbol{\hat{\lambda}}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}})^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q}\right]=\sum_{\alpha,\beta=x,y,z}\hat{\lambda}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2};\alpha\beta}\mathcal{S}_{n\mathbf{k}}^{q;\alpha\beta}.

(242)

Moreover, given
(i)
our choice of isotropic elasticity,
(ii)
a given Hall plane
\mu\nu
and perpendicular Hall axis,

\rho
,
(iii)

\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=\ell
,
\hat{\lambda}
is a function of

\Lambda_{1,..,7}^{\xi(^{\prime})}
contains
72
values, which can be
parametrized by a single index
i=0,..,71
through, e.g.
i=36\xi+18\xi^{\prime}+9\ell+3\alpha+\beta
if we identify
(x,y,z)
with

(0,1,2)
for
\alpha
and
\beta
,
\mathcal{S}
is a
complex function of
n,\rho,\mathbf{k},q,\ell,\alpha,\beta
.

E.4
Details of the derivation of the general forms of the scaling relations

Here we give details about the results and calculations in Sec.
V.5.5
.

E.4.1
Dimensionless functions

The functional forms of the scaling functions introduced in
Eq. (
110
) are

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\eta}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(|\sin\theta|+\eta\upsilon^{-1}X(\tilde{y})\right),

(243)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}^{\pm\eta}_{\ell}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sin^{2}\theta X^{2}(\tilde{y})+\upsilon^{-2}\pm 2\eta|\sin\theta|\upsilon^{-1}X(\tilde{y})},

with

X(\tilde{y})=\sqrt{1+4\frac{\tilde{y}^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta-\upsilon^{-2}}},

(244)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm f}}_{\eta}^{s=1}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta\left(\upsilon^{-2}-\sin^{2}\theta-4|\tilde{y}|^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm f}}_{\eta}^{s=-1}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta,1}\Theta\left(\sin^{2}\theta-\upsilon^{-2}\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{J}_{D}^{s}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{r=\pm 1}\frac{s^{(r-1)/2}r\tilde{c}_{r}(\tilde{y})}{\sqrt{\tilde{c}_{r}(\tilde{y})^{2}+\tilde{y}^{2}}}\right|^{-1}.

(245)

Inserting the expressions for
\mathcal{L}
and
{\rm F}
into that of

\mathcal{B}
, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{n,\ell\ell|+s-}_{\mathbf{k};-\mathbf{p}^{(\eta)}_{\ell,\mathbf{k}}(y)+\frac{\mathbf{k}}{2}}

(246)

\displaystyle=\frac{-i}{2\sqrt{2M_{\rm uc}}}\sum_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}n_{0}^{-\xi-\xi^{\prime}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=x,y,z}\hat{\lambda}^{\ell\ell;\alpha\beta}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}\;i^{\overline{\xi}}(si)^{\overline{\xi}^{\prime}}(-1)^{(\overline{\xi}+\overline{\xi}^{\prime})\ell}

\displaystyle\quad\frac{k^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\beta}+k^{\beta}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\omega_{n\mathbf{k}}}}(\chi\Omega_{\ell\mathbf{p}_{\ell\mathbf{k}}^{(\eta)}}(y))^{\xi-\frac{1}{2}}(\chi\Omega_{\ell\mathbf{p}_{\ell\mathbf{k}}^{(-\eta)}}(y))^{\xi^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}}

because
F_{\xi q\ell}(-\mathbf{p})=F_{\xi q\ell}(\mathbf{p})
. Then,
for
\delta_{\ell}=0
, and defining

\hat{k}^{\alpha}=(\mathbf{\hat{k}})_{\alpha}
, the

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}
introduced in Eq. (
110
) are:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{nn}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{is\chi^{-1}v_{\rm m}^{-1}}{2\sqrt{2M_{\rm uc}v_{\rm ph}}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=x,y,z}\hat{\lambda}^{\ell\ell;\alpha\beta}_{00}\;(\hat{k}^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\beta}+\hat{k}^{\beta}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\alpha})

(247)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(\tilde{\Omega}^{\eta}(\tilde{y})\tilde{\Omega}^{-\eta}(\tilde{y}))^{-\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{mm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-in_{0}^{-2}\chi v_{\rm m}}{2\sqrt{2M_{\rm uc}v_{\rm ph}}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=x,y,z}\hat{\lambda}^{\ell\ell;\alpha\beta}_{11}\;(\hat{k}^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\beta}+\hat{k}^{\beta}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\alpha})

(248)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(\tilde{\Omega}^{\eta}(\tilde{y})\tilde{\Omega}^{-\eta}(\tilde{y}))^{\frac{1}{2}},

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{mn}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\ell}n_{0}^{-1}}{2\sqrt{2M_{\rm uc}v_{\rm ph}}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=x,y,z}(\hat{k}^{\alpha}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\beta}+\hat{k}^{\beta}\varepsilon_{n\mathbf{k}}^{\alpha})

(249)

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\lambda}^{\ell\ell;\alpha\beta}_{01}\;(\tilde{\Omega}^{\eta}(\tilde{y}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\tilde{\Omega}^{-\eta}(\tilde{y}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+s\;\hat{\lambda}^{\ell\ell;\alpha\beta}_{10}\;(\tilde{\Omega}^{(\eta)}(\tilde{y}))^{\frac{1}{2}}(\tilde{\Omega}^{(-\eta)}(\tilde{y}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right].

E.4.2
Details of the behavior of the scaling function
\rm F

Here we derive the behavior of the scaling functions
{\rm F}_{x}(\varkappa,\upsilon,\theta)
defined in Eq. (
112
) at
small and large
\varkappa
.

Let us first consider the large-
\varkappa
limit. The hyperbolic sines in the
denominator of the integrand grow exponentially in this limit (because
the two

\tilde{\Omega}^{\pm\eta}_{\ell}(\tilde{y})
functions cannot be
simultaneously be made to vanish), so that they can be approximated by
their leading exponential forms. An application of the saddle point
method then shows that the integral is dominated by region around

\tilde{y}=0
, and is exponentially suppressed for large
\varkappa
.
For
\upsilon|\sin\theta|>1
, i.e. when the azimuthal angle of

\mathbf{k}
is smaller than
\upsilon^{-1}
, this suppression exceeds
the exponential growth of the
\sinh\varkappa/2
prefactor, and the
scaling function decays exponentially:

{\rm F}_{x}(\varkappa,\upsilon>|\sin\theta|^{-1})\underset{\varkappa\gg 1}{\sim}\exp\left[\frac{\varkappa}{2}(1-\upsilon|\sin\theta|)\right].

(250)

For smaller angles where
\upsilon|\sin\theta|<1
,
{\rm F}
does not
decay exponentially in the large
\kappa
limit. Here
Eq. (
250
) is correct to exponential accuracy, i.e. it is
asymptotically correct for
\ln({\rm F})
at large
\varkappa
. To
this accuracy, the asymptotics are independent of
x
.

Now consider the small
\varkappa
limit. The naïve result for the
scaling function is obtained by
expanding both the hyperbolic sine in the numerator and the two in the
denominator of the integrand around zero leads to

\displaystyle{\rm F}^{(s)}_{x}(\varkappa\ll 1,\upsilon,\theta)\underset{?}{\sim}\frac{1}{\varkappa}\frac{(3-s)\mathfrak{a}^{2}}{2\pi v_{\rm m}\hbar^{2}\upsilon^{2}}

(251)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\text{d}\tilde{y}\sum_{\eta}\tilde{\rm f}_{\eta}^{s}(\tilde{y})\tilde{J}_{D}^{s}(\tilde{y})\sum_{\ell}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{x}(\tilde{y})}{\tilde{\Omega}^{+\eta}_{\ell}(\tilde{y})\tilde{\Omega}^{-\eta}_{\ell}(\tilde{y})}.

This expression is correct provided the integral in the second line
converges. The convergence is problematic only at large
\tilde{y}

for the case
s=-1
(in the case
s=1
, the integral is confined by the

\tilde{{\rm f}}^{s=1}
factor to a finite domain).
In this limit the Jacobean
\tilde{J}^{s=-1}
grows linearly in

\tilde{y}
as does
\tilde{\Omega}
, while the factor

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{x}(\tilde{y})
behaves as
\tilde{y}^{2x}
. As
a result, the integral converges for the case
x=-1
and the

1/\varkappa
scaling is correct in this case. In the cases
x=0,1
, the
integral is logarithmically and quadratically divergent at large

\tilde{y}
, respectively.

In the latter two cases, we must reconsider the naïve result in
Eq. (
251
). The divergence in this equation is an artificial
result because the hyperbolic sines in the original expression in Eq. (
112
) grow rapidly once
\tilde{\Omega}>\varkappa^{-1}
and ensure convergence
of the integral (i.e. the large
\tilde{y}>|\upsilon\varkappa|^{-1}

contribution is negligible). Proper behavior is restored for small
\varkappa
by
simply using the expanded form of Eq. (
251
) but only
integrating up to an upper cutoff
|\tilde{y}|<|\varkappa\upsilon|^{-1}
. This regulates the divergences and one
obtains additional
\ln(1/\varkappa)
and
\varkappa^{-2}
factors
multiplying the
1/\varkappa
form for the cases
x=0,1
,
respectively. Collecting the above results we see that

{\rm F}^{(-1)}_{x}(\varkappa)\underset{\varkappa\ll 1}{\sim}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{\varkappa^{3}}&x=1\\
\frac{\ln(1/\varkappa)}{\varkappa}&x=0\\
\frac{1}{\varkappa}&x=-1\end{array}.\right.

(252)

This function is non-zero for
|\upsilon\sin\theta|>1
, while
F^{(+1)}

is non-zero when
|\upsilon\sin\theta|<1
.

In the latter case, as mentioned above, the integral over
\tilde{y}
always converges because
the set of integration is an ellipse instead of a half-hyperbola. Therefore the naïve scaling is the correct one and the

{\rm F}^{(+1)}_{x}(\varkappa)\underset{\varkappa\ll 1}{\sim}1/\varkappa
behavior holds for all
x
.

Appendix F
Application—further physical details

F.1
Microscopic derivation of the coupling constants

We consider the most general coupling between the strain tensor and bilinears of
the
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}
fields, exhibiting all the symmetries allowed by
the crystal symmetry group in the paramagnetic phase:
the
D_{4h}
tetragonal point-group; translations of one unit cell
—which forbids interactions of the
{\rm m}_{a}{\rm n}_{b}
type;
and time-reversal.
The corresponding hamiltonian density (where for the sake of readability we have replaced
n_{0}\rightarrow 1
) reads:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\rm tetra}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{1}\Big({\rm m}_{x}{\rm m}_{x}\mathcal{E}^{xx}+{\rm m}_{y}{\rm m}_{y}\mathcal{E}^{yy}\Big)+\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{1}\Big({\rm n}_{x}{\rm n}_{x}\mathcal{E}^{xx}+{\rm n}_{y}{\rm n}_{y}\mathcal{E}^{yy}\Big)+\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{5}{\rm m}_{z}{\rm m}_{z}\mathcal{E}^{zz}+\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{5}{\rm n}_{z}{\rm n}_{z}\mathcal{E}^{zz}

(256)

\displaystyle+\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{2}\Big({\rm m}_{x}{\rm m}_{x}\mathcal{E}^{yy}+{\rm m}_{y}{\rm m}_{y}\mathcal{E}^{xx}\Big)+\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{2}\Big({\rm n}_{x}{\rm n}_{x}\mathcal{E}^{yy}+{\rm n}_{y}{\rm n}_{y}\mathcal{E}^{xx}\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big(\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{3}{\rm m}_{z}{\rm m}_{z}+\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{3}{\rm n}_{z}{\rm n}_{z}\Big)\Big(\mathcal{E}^{xx}+\mathcal{E}^{yy}\Big)+\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{4}\Big({\rm m}_{x}{\rm m}_{x}+{\rm m}_{y}{\rm m}_{y}\Big)\mathcal{E}^{zz}+\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{4}\Big({\rm n}_{x}{\rm n}_{x}+{\rm n}_{y}{\rm n}_{y}\Big)\mathcal{E}^{zz}

\displaystyle+4\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{6}{\rm m}_{x}{\rm m}_{y}\mathcal{E}^{xy}+4\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{6}{\rm n}_{x}{\rm n}_{y}\mathcal{E}^{xy}+4\Lambda^{(\rm m)}_{7}\Big({\rm m}_{x}{\rm m}_{z}\mathcal{E}^{xz}+{\rm m}_{y}{\rm m}_{z}\mathcal{E}^{yz}\Big)+4\Lambda^{(\rm n)}_{7}({\rm n}_{x}{\rm n}_{z}\mathcal{E}^{xz}+{\rm n}_{y}{\rm n}_{z}\mathcal{E}^{yz}).

We now propose a microscopic origin to the
\Lambda^{(\xi)}_{I}
coefficients appearing in it.

We start from a generic spin exchange hamiltonian of the form

H_{\rm ex}=\sum_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\sum_{a,b}S_{\mathbf{R}}^{a}J_{\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}^{ab}S_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}^{b},

(257)

where
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
indicate the actual locations of the sites in the

distorted
lattice, and each sum spans the whole distorted lattice.

We then express
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{r}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathbf{r}}
, where

\mathbf{r}
belongs to the undistorted lattice and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\mathbf{r}}
is the displacement field
at site
\mathbf{r}
. Taylor-expanding the coefficients
J_{\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}^{ab}
with respect to the displacement
field (and identifying
S^{a}_{\mathbf{R}}=S^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}
), we thus obtain

H_{\rm ex}=H_{\rm ex}^{0}+H^{\prime}_{\rm ex}+O(u^{2})
,
where
H_{\rm ex}^{0}=H_{\rm ex}|_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}
and

H^{\prime}_{\rm ex}=\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\sum_{ab,\nu}S^{a}_{\mathbf{r}}\left(u^{\nu}_{\mathbf{r}}-u^{\nu}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\right)\partial_{\eta^{\nu}}J_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{ab}\Big|_{\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}S^{b}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}.

(258)

Then, identifying
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\alpha}u_{\beta}+\partial_{\beta}u_{\alpha})
the symmetric rank-2 elasticity tensor
(i.e. strain tensor), we identify
H^{\prime}_{\rm ex}=H^{\prime}_{ss\varepsilon}+\dots
, where

H^{\prime}_{ss\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\boldsymbol{\eta}}S_{\mathbf{r}+\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{a}S_{\mathbf{r}}^{b}\left[\eta_{\alpha}\partial_{\eta^{\beta}}+\eta_{\beta}\partial_{\eta^{\alpha}}\right]J^{ab}\Big|_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\,\mathcal{E}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{r})+\dots,

(259)

where
a,b=x,y,z
is a spin axis index,
\alpha,\beta=x,y,z

is a spatial index, and “
+\dots
” encompasses terms featuring
\omega_{\alpha\beta}

the anti-symmetric rank-2 elasticity tensor, as well as higher-order derivatives
of the displacement field.

Note that in this microscopic derivation, we identify
S^{a}_{\mathbf{R}}\mapsto S^{a}_{{\mathbf{r}}}
.
In fact, also expanding the
magnetization fields
(and not only the magnetic exchange)
with respect to displacement yields an interaction term which is
formally
of the same order as that derived here.
However, magnetization in an ordered magnet is a slow variable, while
J
varies over distances of the order of the lattice parameter
\mathfrak{a}
,
therefore such terms are
quantitatively
much smaller by a factor
O(k_{B}T\mathfrak{a}/v_{\rm m})
,
both within and beyond the Born-Oppenheimer approximation.

Finally, we take the particular case of a square lattice with tetragonal symmetry,
and describe the spins in terms of
\rm m,n
fields as in the main text,
namely
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{r}}=(-1)^{\mathbf{r}}\mu_{0}\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{r})+\mathfrak{a}^{2}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{r})
.
We identify
H^{\prime}_{ss\varepsilon}=\sum_{\mathbf{r}}\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\rm tetra}(\mathbf{r})+\dots

where “
+\dots
” is made of rapidly oscillating (time-reversal breaking) terms,
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\rm tetra}
is as displayed in Eq. (
256
), with identification

\displaystyle\Lambda_{ab}^{({\rm m}),\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\left(\eta_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}+\eta_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\right)J^{ab}\Big|_{\boldsymbol{\eta}},

(260)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{ab}^{({\rm n}),\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}e^{i\boldsymbol{\pi}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\left(\eta_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}+\eta_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\right)J^{ab}\Big|_{\boldsymbol{\eta}},

(261)

where the sum over
\boldsymbol{\eta}
spans the whole direct (two-dimensional square)
lattice, and

\boldsymbol{\pi}=\left(\frac{\pi}{\mathfrak{a}},\frac{\pi}{\mathfrak{a}}\right)
with
\mathfrak{a}

the square lattice parameter.

F.2
Contributions to intervalley couplings

In the main text, the
n_{a},m_{a}
fields live in the valleys identified by:

\displaystyle\ell=0:

\displaystyle\qquad n_{y},m_{z}

\displaystyle\ell=1:

\displaystyle\qquad n_{z},m_{y}.

(262)

Therefore, intervalley couplings are of the form

\lambda_{ab;\xi\xi^{\prime}}
with

\delta_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}+\delta_{ab}=1
. More explicitly, using
Eq. (
73
), they are:

\displaystyle\lambda_{yz;00}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{yz}^{({\rm n}),\alpha\beta},

(263)

\displaystyle\lambda_{yz;11}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{yz}^{({\rm m}),\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle\lambda_{yy;01}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{n_{0}}\left[m^{y}_{0}\Lambda^{({\rm m}),\alpha\beta}_{yx}+m^{z}_{0}\Lambda_{zx}^{({\rm m}),\alpha\beta}+m^{y}_{0}\Lambda^{({\rm n}),\alpha\beta}_{yx}\right],

\displaystyle\lambda_{zz;01}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{n_{0}}\left[m^{z}_{0}\Lambda^{({\rm m}),\alpha\beta}_{zx}+m^{y}_{0}\Lambda_{yx}^{({\rm m}),\alpha\beta}+m^{z}_{0}\Lambda^{({\rm n}),\alpha\beta}_{zx}\right].

Also recall from Eq. (
V.3.1
) that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{yx}^{(\xi),xy}=\Lambda_{yx}^{(\xi),yx}=\Lambda_{xy}^{(\xi),xy}=\Lambda_{xy}^{(\xi),yx}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{6}^{(\xi)},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{zx}^{(\xi),xz}=\Lambda_{zx}^{(\xi),zx}=\Lambda_{yz}^{(\xi),yz}=\Lambda_{yz}^{(\xi),zy}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{7}^{(\xi)},

(264)

and all other values of
\alpha,\beta
yield 0 for this set of lower
indices. From this, it is clear that the
\Lambda_{7}^{(\xi)}
couplings
always mix valleys, regardless of
\boldsymbol{m}_{0}
, and contribute a

\lambda_{yz;\xi\xi}
term. This intervalley coupling is a
small contribution which does not contribute to
\mathcal{T}

breaking. Meanwhile, the
\mathcal{T}
-odd
\lambda_{yy;01}
and

\lambda_{zz;01}
intervalley couplings both contain contributions
from both
\Lambda_{7}^{(\xi)}m_{0}^{z}
and
\Lambda_{6}^{(\xi)}m_{0}^{y}
.

F.3
Derivation of the gaps from a sigma model

Here we provide a heuristic microscopic argument for expressing the gaps in terms of spin-spin couplings.
We ignore spin-lattice coupling, and just consider corrections to the isotropic Heisenberg model. We
assume the addition of a term of the XXZ anisotropy form:

H_{\rm XXZ}=gJ\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}\left(2S_{i}^{z}S_{j}^{z}-S_{i}^{x}S_{j}^{x}-S_{i}^{y}S_{j}^{y}\right).

(265)

This is to be added to the isotropic Heisenberg model,
along with a Zeeman coupling to the transverse field.

Carrying out the long-wavelength expansion in terms of
\mathbf{m}
and
\mathbf{n}

fields, we obtain the corrected potential part (i.e. without gradient terms) of the
nonlinear sigma-model Eq. (
50
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rm NLS}^{g,h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\chi}|\mathbf{m}|^{2}+2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\left(2\text{m}_{z}^{2}-\text{m}_{x}^{2}-\text{m}_{y}^{2}\right)

(266)

\displaystyle-2gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left(2\text{n}_{z}^{2}-\text{n}_{x}^{2}-\text{n}_{y}^{2}\right)-h_{y}\text{m}_{y}-h_{z}\text{m}_{z}.

Note that the first term includes an
\text{m}_{x}^{2}
term, which is
absent in the quadratic expansion describing linear spin waves in the main
text. Indeed this term is higher order in the small fluctuations around an

x
-ordered state when carrying out a zero field spin
wave expansion, which was the case in the main text where the external field
had already been integrated out to yield the
\text{n}_{a}\text{n}_{b}

mass term. We also included an external uniform field which
lies in the
y-z
plane.

Expanding around the
x
-ordered state, using that
\text{m}_{x}=-\mathsf{m}_{y}\mathsf{n}_{y}-\mathsf{m}_{z}\mathsf{n}_{z}
and

\text{n}_{x}=1-\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{n}_{y}^{2}+\mathsf{n}_{z}^{2}+\frac{1}{n_{0}^{2}}(\mathsf{m}_{y}^{2}+\mathsf{m}_{z}^{2}))
, yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rm NLS}^{g,h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\chi}\left(\mathsf{m}_{y}^{2}+\mathsf{m}_{z}^{2}\right)+2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\left(2\mathsf{m}_{z}^{2}-\mathsf{m}_{y}^{2}\right)-2gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left(2\mathsf{n}_{z}^{2}-\mathsf{n}_{y}^{2}\right)-h_{y}\mathsf{m}_{y}-h_{z}\mathsf{m}_{z}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{2\chi}-2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\right)\left(\mathsf{m}_{y}^{2}\mathsf{n}_{y}^{2}+\mathsf{m}_{z}^{2}\mathsf{n}_{z}^{2}+2\mathsf{m}_{y}\mathsf{m}_{z}\mathsf{n}_{y}\mathsf{n}_{z}\right)+2gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left[1-\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{n}_{y}^{2}+\mathsf{n}_{z}^{2}+\frac{1}{n_{0}^{2}}(\mathsf{m}_{y}^{2}+\mathsf{m}_{z}^{2}))\right]^{2}.

(267)

Note that the first term on the second line is of the form

(\mathbf{m}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\perp})^{2}
, where the
\perp
indicates the
components of the vectors normal to the ordering direction. Since we
in the next step shift the magnetization by its value induced by the
field, this is proportional to
(\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{n})^{2}
, as is postulated
in the main text on symmetry grounds.

We now show this explicitly. We shift the definition

\mathsf{m}_{a}=m_{a}+\chi_{a}h_{a}
for
a=y,z
,
and expand the result to quadratic order in
m,n
. Here

\chi_{z}=(1/\chi+4gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2})^{-1}
and

\chi_{y}=(1/\chi-2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2})^{-1}
.

This gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\rm NLS}^{g,h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\chi}\left(m_{y}^{2}+m_{z}^{2}\right)+2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\left(2m_{z}^{2}-m_{y}^{2}\right)-2gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left(2n_{z}^{2}-n_{y}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{2\chi}-2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\right)\left(\chi_{y}^{2}h_{y}^{2}n_{y}^{2}+\chi_{z}^{2}h_{z}^{2}n_{z}^{2}+2\chi_{y}\chi_{z}h_{y}h_{z}n_{y}n_{z}\right)-2gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left[n_{y}^{2}+n_{z}^{2}+\cdots\right],

(268)

where the ‘
\cdots
’ in the last brackets account for terms higher order in
field, magnetization fluctuations, etc.

The anisotropy coefficients, denoted by
\Gamma_{ab}
in the text, can now be extracted.
The terms in
\mathcal{H}_{\rm NLS}^{g,h}
which are quadratic in the
n_{y},n_{z}
fields read

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{nn}=\chi_{y}^{2}h_{y}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\chi}-2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\right)n_{y}^{2}+\left[\chi_{z}^{2}h_{z}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\chi}-2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\right)-6gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\right]n_{z}^{2}+2\chi_{y}\chi_{z}h_{y}h_{z}\left(\frac{1}{2\chi}-2gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}\right)n_{y}n_{z}.

(269)

Note that the two terms proportional to
n_{y}^{2}
from the right-most
contributions on each line of Eq. (
F.3
) above canceled. That
means the the coefficient of
n_{y}^{2}
in Eq. (
269
) vanishes if

h_{y}=0
. This occurs because of Goldstone’s theorem and the assumed
XXZ form of the anisotropy: if the field is purely along the
z

direction, XY symmetry of the Hamiltonian under rotations about the

z
axis is preserved, and this makes one of the spin wave modes
remain gapless. Conversely, for a field along the
y
direction, and
in the presence of anisotropy, both modes are generally gapped.

We can simplify the above expression if we assume
|g|\ll 1
, which
means
\chi_{y}^{-1}\approx\chi_{z}^{-1}\approx\chi^{-1}=4\mathfrak{a}^{2}J

and therefore
1/\chi\gg gJ\mathfrak{a}^{2}
; hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{nn}\approx\frac{\chi h_{y}^{2}}{2}n_{y}^{2}+\left[\frac{\chi h_{z}^{2}}{2}-6gJ\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\right]n_{z}^{2}+\chi h_{y}h_{z}n_{y}n_{z}.

(270)

The above shows that if
h_{z}
is small or zero, stability
requires
g<0
. This can be understood from the fact that, if the field
is along
y
, then
H_{\rm XXZ}
is the only term, in the pure spin hamiltonian,
breaking explicitly the
O(2)
symmetry in the
x-z
plane.
It should therefore favor antiferromagnetic alignment along the
x
axis,
which is the initial assumption of this derivation. It also proves the
\frac{\chi}{2}

prefactor used in the main text.

The coefficients in Eq. (
270
) give contributions to
\Gamma_{yy}
,
\Gamma_{zz}

and
\Gamma_{yz}
, respectively. In this Appendix, as opposed to the more general expressions
given in the main text, we assume they are the only contribution.

Since taking the magnetic field purely along one of the two axes
y,z
guarantees that
\Gamma_{yz}=0
,
so that (as explained in the main text) the two magnon valleys are independent,
let us assume that the field is along the
y
axis.
Then one gap is
\Delta_{1}=|h_{y}|
, the Zeeman energy associated
with the field along
y
. The other gap gets contributions both from
the anisotropy and the Zeeman energy associated
with the field along
z
.

Note that the anisotropy-induced gap involves the square
root of the anisotropy, i.e.
\Delta_{0}\big|_{h_{z}=0}=4\sqrt{3|g|}J\mu_{0}
,
which is not necessarily
very
small for reasonably small values of
g
.
